decision to proceed versus watchful waiting. Syndromic craniofacial syndromes frequently need an early midface distraction/advancement (Lefort III or monobloc) secondary
to severe obstructive sleep apnea and/or risk of permanent
eye damage due to severe exophthalmos (Figs. 2.14 and
2.15). Even when performed early (5–10 years of age) an
excellent and stable result may be obtained. Overcorrection

35

is encouraged due to near cessation of anterior maxillary
growth following surgical correction and late phase mandibular growth resulting in a class III occlusal relationship
which will invariably necessitate a further and definitive
double jaw orthognathic surgery once the patient reaches
skeletal maturity.112,154
Stability of the orbital correction achieved with an early
Lefort III correction is initially excellent; however, at 10-year
follow-up, phenotypic relapse had occurred secondary to
continued normal anterior growth of the globe, orbital rim
remodeling in an inferior and posterior direction, and failure of the midface to appropriately grow.112 Overcorrection
is recommended to help combat these sequelae but the need
for additional augmentation is likely.
Generally, for a patient with Apert syndrome, a facial
bipartition is needed to address the hypertelorism and transverse deficiency at the maxillary dental arch as compared
with the mandibular dental arch. In a Crouzon patient,
a Lefort III advancement (distraction versus traditional
advancement with bone grafting) will be needed2 ( Fig.
2.15). A final orthognathic surgery is needed once skeletal
maturity is obtained to address the residual open bite deformity (Apert syndrome) and maxillary mandibular discrepancies (Apert/Crouzon/Pfeifer syndrome) (Fig. 2.17A–E).
Apert, Crouzon, Pfeiffer, and Saethre-Chotzen patients
suffer from severe obstructive nasal stenosis and frequently
have undergone a Lefort III which results in a significant
beaking deformity of the nasal dorsum. A definitive nasoseptorhinoplasty is recommended once the patient reaches
skeletal maturity and after correction of maxillomandibular
deformities (Fig. 2.17A-E).

Summary
In syndromic craniosynostosis, fusion of facial bony sutures
in combination with fusion of cranial sutures leads to the
dysmorphic facies that are seen in this patient population.
For this reason, it is unlikely that early release of cranial
sutures will lead to complete correction of facial deformities.
It will likely lessen the facial deformity that results secondary to fusion of the cranial sutures. More research would be
beneficial in this area to determine the effect of early suture
release on the facial deformities of this patient population
Comprehensive craniofacial care by an approved team
is essential in the treatment of patients with complex craniofacial syndromes (Table 2.7). Appropriate care includes:
yearly ophthalmologic evaluation for papilledema, audiological care, speech evaluations, yearly MRIs, annual clinical
evaluation for sleep-disordered breathing and developmental delays, and preoperative MR venography.136,153

3

Background and
Management Principles
DAVID F. JIMENEZ, MD, FACS

C

raniosynostosis, defined as the untimely and premature closure of a calvarial suture in an infant, leads
to well-defined and described phenotypic deformational changes of the neocranium, endocranium, or both.
The first recorded description in modern times is ascribed
to Otto1 in 1830. Twenty-one years later, Rudolf Virchow,
a German pathologist, developed a classification system for
the phenotypes of deformities associated with craniosynostosis.2 The underlying principle in his theory is that bone
growth takes place perpendicular to the suture, and premature closure leads to defined skull shapes based on compensatory brain growth in other areas of the skull. Dr. Odilon
Marc Lannelongue, a French surgeon, was the first to report
a surgical correction for craniosynostosis in 1890.3 His procedure consisted of two parallel strip craniectomies lateral to
the midline, leaving a strip of bone over the sagittal sinus.
Lannelongue performed this operation on 59 patients and
reported only one death. This work was quickly adopted by
other surgeons, including Dr. L.C. Lane of San Francisco
in 1892. Since the introduction of the original surgery in
1890, a multitude of surgeries and procedures have been
developed and instituted throughout the world. In 1967,
French surgeon Paul Tessier performed the first “craniofacial” operation and thus instituted and pioneered the discipline of craniofacial surgery. Subsequently, craniofacial
techniques championed by Tessier were used for the treatment of craniosynostosis. Many different types of extensive
reconstruction procedures are now classified under the term
“calvarial vault remodeling” (CVR) and have become the
standard of care for managing this condition.

Epidemiology and Other General Facts
It has been generally estimated that craniosynostosis develops in about 1 in 2500 births worldwide.4 The incidence
varies according to the affected suture. Prevalence is approximately 3.1 to 6.4 in 10,000 live births and reportedly rising.
A 2016 study in the Netherlands found a prevalence of 7.2
per 10,000 live births.5 This study also showed an annual
increase prevalence for total craniosynostosis of 12.5%, sagittal (+11.7%), and metopic (+20.5%) from 1997 to 2013.

Another study in Australia indicated the prevalence there
to be 3.1 per 10,000 live births and an overall increase in
incidence on nonsyndromic craniosynostosis of 2.5% per
year. Metopic synostosis was found to have increased by
7.1% over a 25-year period.6 It has been our experience
over the last 25 years that there has indeed been an increase
in the overall incidence of metopic synostosis, with this
suture accounting for the second highest number of cases
after sagittal synostosis. It is also estimated that up to 20%
of the cases are caused by specific single gene mutations or
chromosomal abnormalities. The most commonly mutated
genes include FGFR2, FGFR3, TWIST 1, and EFNB1.
Most genetically determined craniosynostosis is characterized with an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, with
half of the cases being new mutations and most commonly
present in syndromic patients. Craniosynostosis has been
described in more than 150 syndromes but most frequently,
it has been associated with Apert, Crouzon, Pfeiffer and
Saethre-Chotzen syndromes.7,8
In a 10-year prospective study,9 a genetic diagnosis was
achieved in 21% of cases, with 86% being single gene disorders and 15% being chromosome abnormalities. FGFR2
accounted for 32% of all genetic cases, FGFR3 for 25%,
TWIST for 19%, and EFNB1 for 7%. Other associations
found included the following genes with the corresponding syndromes: FGFR1 (Pfeiffer syndrome), POR (AntleyBixler syndrome), RAB23 (Carpenter syndrome), ESC02
(Roberts syndrome), GL13 (Greig syndrome), JAG1 (Alagille syndrome), KRAS (Noonan syndrome), FGFR2 (Apert
syndrome), and FRFR3 (Muenke and Crouzon syndromes).
A persistent debate in the literature has been whether
single suture craniosynostosis causes decreased intracranial
volume and elevations in intracranial pressure (ICP). Several studies have found little difference in the intracranial
volume among the different types of craniosynostosis10,11;
as such, craniosynostosis has not been found to be associated with low or lower intracranial volumes. This is most
likely due to compensatory brain growth in other areas secondary to other patent sutures. Likewise, hydrocephalus has
not been found to have a higher incidence in patients with
craniosynostosis,12 with an estimated incidence of 0.28%

44

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 3

in infants with nonsyndromic synostosis. This is the same
incidence of hydrocephalus and shunting seen in the general
population. However, even though craniosynostosis has not
been shown to be associated with decreases in intracranial
volumes, multiple studies have shown a positive correlation
with increased intracranial pressure. A study which defined
elevated ICP as greater than 15 mmHg, found an incidence
of intracranial hypertension of 20%, with a large number
being found in syndromic cases.13 Another study found
an incidence of intracranial hypertension in 30% of the
affected children and included a 14% incidence in infants
with single suture synostosis.14 Thompson also found an
incidence of increased ICP in 15% of single suture synostosis and 24% in nonsyndromic craniosynostosis.15 These
findings raise the question of the need for early rather than
delayed surgery in these patients. It has been our experience
with the use of minimally invasive early surgery to find that
most of our parents consistently report improvement in the
child’s behavior and development soon after surgery. Anecdotally, we are told that the baby is no longer irritable and
fuzzy, that the child sleeps and eats better. Motor function
has also repeatedly been found to improve. We have also
found this to be the case on many postoperative follow-up
clinic visits. We did (unpublished) a prospective study looking at irritability and fuzziness of our endoscopically treated
patients, assessing frequency and severity on a scale from 0
to 5. Parents were asked to rate their children before and at
1 and 2 months after surgery. Our results indicated a significant decrease in both parameters as related to frequency and
severity (P < .001). These findings seem to indicate that the
cause of the patient’s irritability immediately subsided after
surgery. That would not be as evident with the much more
invasive and traumatic CVR surgery.

Brain Growth and Timing of Surgery
Brain growth and development is a complex and dynamic
process in the first years of life. Total brain volume increases
by 101% in the first year of life and is followed by a 15%
increase in the second year16; grey matter growth increases
by 149% in the first year and accounts for the majority of
cerebral growth. The cerebellar volume increases by 240%
during the first year. As such, premature sutural closure can
have significant effects on a rapidly growing brain, particularly in the first year of life. The ultimate effects of craniosynostosis on overall cognition and neurodevelopment are
not well known. A number of studies have been done looking at this issue, yet no clear consensus can be had. Some
studies have looked at maldevelopment of speech, behavior,
and cognition. One study showed a 47% rate of neurocognitive developmental abnormalities in children aged 5 years
or older who had undergone corrective surgery.17 Another
study found a 37% rate of speech delay, language, and
cognitive impairment in children with nontreated sagittal
synostosis.18 Thus, it has been difficult to discern if the neurodevelopmental problems arise from the craniosynostosis
itself or the surgery used to correct the condition.

Background and Management Principles

45

Although our team performed a number of open procedures over the years to treat craniosynostosis, we became
particularly disillusioned with the lack of consistency of
the short- and long-term results. Whether similar procedures such as pi or reverse pi procedures were done, or more
extensive calvarial vault expansions and reconstruction such
as the Marchac transpositions, the results were consistently
suboptimal and inconsistent. The use of distractors did not
help improve the poor results of single isolated craniosynostosis. It was this lack of reliable and consistent results
that led us to rethink and reevaluate our surgical approach.
Hence, relying on the concept of extreme brain growth during the first year of life, coupled with the belief that the
brain is the primary driver of head and facial growth, we
conceptualized, in the mid-1990s, that ultra-early release of
the closed suture should allow the brain to develop normally
and reshape the misshapen face and cranium. Contrary to
Moss’ theory, which in essence states that the skull base
plays the primary role in the pathophysiology of craniosynostosis, we believe that the skull base changes are only
secondary to calvarial sutural stenosis. Furthermore, early
release of the suture should solve the primary problem in
isolated, nonsyndromic synostosis. The problem remained
how to do surgery safely in very young children only several weeks of age. Utilizing concepts learned from endoscopic neurosurgical procedures, we decided to apply these
concepts to treating craniosynostosis infants. Our original
goals included minimizing the following factors: (1) surgical exposure; (2) surgical time; (3) intraoperative blood loss;
(4) blood transfusion rates; (5) length of hospitalization;
and (6) complications; and most importantly, to improve
outcome consistency and better long-term results. The techniques described in this book are the work done by our team
over the last 25 years using these principles and techniques.
The results, we believe, speak for themselves.
Our current treatment protocol calls for treating affected
infants as soon as the diagnosis is made and surgery can
be arranged. The most ideal time is 8 weeks of age. This is
an ideal time for a number of reasons, including the fact
that the calvarial bone is very thin, malleable, and easy to
cut. The diploë is almost nonexistent or very small and as
such, bone bleeding is minimal when the osteotomies are
performed. Because of the rapid brain growth that occurs
during this period, the correction of the deformity happens
rather quickly. This is of particular importance in a coronal
synostosis in which the face, orbit, forehead, nose, and skull
base are all affected. Early release allows the brain to correct
all these deformities, as can be seen in the outcomes shown
in that Chapter 9. In patients with metopic synostosis and
significant trigonocephaly and hypotelorism, both of these
deformities are completely corrected when infants are operated on at an early age. In the cases of bicoronal synostosis,
the forehead and facial advancement can be seen by the end
of the first year of life. Even in cases of syndromic bicoronal
synostosis, early bilateral coronal sutural release negates the
need for a fronto-orbital advancement. Our original recommendation for timing of surgery was 3 months of age or

booksmedicos.org

46 C HA P T ER 3

Background and Management Principles

younger. Subsequent experience allowed us to recommend
surgery up to 6 months of age. In efforts to set an upper
limit, we continued to expand our timeline, and in selected
cases, we operate on children of 9 or 10 months of age and
still achieve superior results to those obtained in patients
operated with CVR techniques.

Postoperative Orthosis Therapy
For thousands of years, indigenous people have used a
number of contraptions and apparatuses to mold the head
of infants in their belief that certain head shapes lead to
increased beauty and power. In intentional cranial deformation as a “process of dynamic distortion of the normal vectors of infantile neurocranial growth through the agency of
externally applied forces”19 a number of constrictive devices
have been utilized and include wooden boards and planks,
cloth ties, bands, manual molding, stones and rocks placed
on cribs. Other appliances have also been tried and successfully used. The reason for these practices includes the goals
of creating class and tribal distinction, enhancing sexual

prowess or intellect, and, in others, intimidating opponents
in battle. The rationale then exists that if a normal head can
be misshaped, can a misshaped head be similarly corrected?
During the treatment of our craniosynostosis patients with
CVR procedures, we began to successfully use molding helmets in efforts to minimize relapse and the development
of postoperative deformity. Our results definitely improved
with the use of postoperative orthoses. When we began to
release stenosed sutures endoscopically, we immediately
began to use these molding orthoses soon after surgery with
great success and have continued to do so until current days.
Notwithstanding this, the use of helmets has generated a
significant amount of controversy and angst amongst craniofacial surgeons treating these patients. Although these
helmets provide the patients with superb results and minimal problems or complications, a lot of resistance is still
present from many surgeons treating these children. It is our
strong belief that these orthoses should be an integral part
of the therapy. We have carefully studied all of our patients
and no brain growth restriction has been found in any of
them (Fig. 3.1). Careful follow up on head circumferences

indicate no deviation from expected isocurves for the individual patient. Complete neuropsychologic testing has not
shown any negative effect of cranial molding.
Insofar as sagittal synostosis is concerned, we have found
empirically in our practice that the dura matter behaves
and is programmed differently than in other suture types.
There appears to be a genetically driven force that returns
the operated cranium to the preoperative scaphocephalic
shape. The traditional frontal bossing, occipital cupping,
and biparietal slanting tend to return soon after the head
has rounded following a release. We believe that is one of
the reasons why strip craniectomies have not been totally
successful in treating the deformity. Following our early
success with helmets and CVR surgeries, we instituted a
molding program which consists of three phases. Phase 1
takes place during the first 2 months and aims to get the
cranial vault into normocephaly with a cephalic index (CI)
in the 70 to 75 range. Phase 2 takes place over the next 4
to 6 months and aims to overcorrect the head shape and
to obtain a CI between 80 and 85+ (Fig. 3.2). Phase 3
takes place until the child is 18 months of age. During
this phase, the child looks absolutely normal but the goal

is to maintain normalcy and fight the genetic force that
leads to decreasing CI. It has been our definite and confirmed experience that whatever the CI of the child is at 18
months of age, that such CI is maintained for the rest of
the patient’s life (longest follow up at 21 years shows that
CI at 21 years of age to be only 2 points less than when the
patient was 18 months). Therefore, we advocate for helmet
use until 18 months of age. We have clearly documented
significant drops in CI when parents stop helmet therapy
between 9 and 12 months of age. This does not apply to
metopic or coronal synostosis. Whenever adequate correction is reached (3–12 months postsurgery), we stop the
helmet therapy (Table 3.1).

Postoperative Management
Following surgery, the patient is taken to the post anesthesia care unit (PACU) and appropriately recovered. In our
institution, we place the patient in the pediatric intensive
care unit (PICU) for overnight observation and on 23-hour
observation status. The patient can also be placed on a
regular ward bed if the nursing staff has been adequately

operating room (OR) before extubation. Depending on the
level, a decision to transfuse or not is made. It is our protocol not to transfuse the patient if the hemoglobin is greater
than 6.5 g/dL or hematocrit is 100% or greater and the
following conditions are met:
1.	The surgeon has secured the surgical site to the point that
endoscopic assessment and visualization of the surgical
field confirms no dural, bony, or scalp bleeding. A Valsalva maneuver documents no bleeding or cerebrospinal
fluid leaks;
2.	In the PACU/PICU, the patient’s oxygen saturation is
greater than 95%;
3.	The patient is not tachycardic;
4.	Patient is not hypotensive.
Using this protocol, we have not transfused a single
patient who left the surgical suite and there have been
no untoward effects or need for emergent readmission or
transfusion. If the patient is nursing and breastfed, he/she
is allowed to nurse immediately after surgery at their own
pace. If taking formula, Pedialyte is first given and if tolerated, diet advanced to full formula ad libitum.
Pain management consists of injecting 0.25% bupivacaine with epinephrine 1:250,000 at 1 cc/Kg. The
surgical incision is infiltrated and nerve blocks done
(supraorbital, auricular, occipital) as indicated for each
suture. Postoperative pain is managed with a combination of intravenous and/or oral acetaminophen, alternating with Ibuprofen every 3 hours on a scheduled basis.
The majority of the pain and discomfort will occur during the first 8 hours after surgery. Thereafter the pain rapidly declines and most, if not all, babies will be pain free
and smiling the morning following surgery when they
are ready for discharge. Breakthrough pain is managed by
intravenous morphine (Table 3.2). Usually, no more than
three doses are needed. The morning following surgery,
the patient is discharged home and the ibuprofen/acetaminophen regimen is continued for the next 72 hours
or until the patient is totally comfortable and pain free.
Facial and swelling and bruisingis typically minimal to
none. (Figs. 3.3, 3.4 and 3.5). The patient is allowed to
rest and recover for 72 hours and then he/she is scanned
for a helmet on the fourth postoperative day (POD). The
patient’s anthropometric data is then set to Orthomerica’s

49

Dose

Schedule Notes

• Fig. 3.3 Minimal cranial or facial swelling on post-operative day 1

following an endoscopic craniectomy for treatment of a right coronal
synostosis.

Orlando manufacturing facility where a custom helmet
is made the same day and overnight delivered for fitting
on POD 5. The next day the patient is seen in clinic for
assessment of proper helmet fitting, wound check, and
Steri-strip removal. If adjustments to the helmet are
deemed necessary, these can be made immediately. Follow up is then done as per orthotic clinic protocol and
with the surgical team when the next new helmet is made
and delivered.

Conclusion
This chapter has given an overview of the management
process for patients with craniosynostosis undergoing
endoscopic assisted surgical release. Specific details will be
presented in each suture chapter. We have had excellent to

Perioperative Logistics
DAVID F. JIMENEZ, MD, FACS

Introduction
As with any other surgical operation, adequate preparation
and planning prior to performing the procedure are crucial
and paramount to obtaining an excellent result and minimizing complications. In the case of minimally invasive procedures with small incisions and narrow working corridors,
said planning and preparation play an even more important
role. This chapter will detail a number of principles that
are common to all of the different procedures for treating
craniosynostosis with endoscopic techniques.

Diagnostic Imaging
Diagnosis of single suture, isolated, nonsyndromic craniosynostosis can be made purely on physical examination and
remains a clinical diagnosis. As such, we believe that obtaining imaging is not an absolute necessity to make the diagnosis, although it can corroborate it. It has been our center’s
approach not to expose the young infants to unnecessary
radiation associated with computed tomograms (CT) and
particularly with the protocol required to do three-dimensional (3-D) reconstructions. Each suture synostosis leads
to the development of a very specific and characteristic phenotype, which can be easily diagnosed. Plain radiographs
of the skull are used sparingly and particularly in situations
where associated positional deformational forces (as seen
with certain sleeping patterns and/or with torticollis) lead to
unusual head shapes. We reserve the use of CT scans to situations: (a) when the diagnosis cannot be easily determined
with physical examination; (b) when there is presence or
suspicion of multiple suture synostosis; or (c) if the patient
has a syndromic phenotypic presentation.

Diagnostic Labs
With an otherwise normal infant, it has been our decadeslong policy not to obtain any serum or blood work prior
to surgery. If the birth, family, and developmental history
indicate no abnormalities, the likelihood of an abnormal
blood volume and/or composition with negative or severe
consequences is extremely low. The only laboratory value

that is obtained prior to commencing the surgical procedure is a hemoglobin and hematocrit (H/H) level either via
direct venipuncture, heel stick, or with an I-STAT handheld
blood analyzer. A more extensive and detailed blood work is
obtained as indicated by family history (i.e., von Willebrand
disease or other coagulopathy). In our experience with over
800 patients, only two infants were ultimately found to have
rare blood abnormalities. Upon completion of surgery, an
immediate postoperative H/H is obtained, and a third one
on the next morning. The immediate postoperative H/H
has been found to be a very reliable predictor of blood loss
and for the need for postoperative blood transfusions. Upon
completion of surgery, extreme care is taken to make sure
that all bony, scalp, dural, and epidural bleeding is stopped
and controlled. As such, the immediate postoperative H/H
is a very reliable indicator of the patient’s red blood cell load
and typically drops very little, and usually by no more than
a gram per deciliter. In the last 24 years of treating these
patients, we have not had the need to readmit and transfuse any patient who was discharged with a hematocrit of
15% or greater, nor have the patients had any consequences
because of this management approach.

Ophthalmologic Evaluation
Our center’s pediatric ophthalmologist evaluates all of our
patients prior to surgery and evaluates for abnormalities that
may be present in the retina, vitreous, lens, cornea, muscular
abnormalities, or orbital imbalance. A full ophthalmological
evaluation takes place 1 or 2 days prior to surgery. Postoperative follow up evaluations are done within 6 months
and 1 year. Autorefraction before and after pupillary dilation is done with Plusoptix or Retinomax vision screener
to measure refractive errors such as myopia, astigmatism,
or hyperopia. Cycloplegic refraction is done with streak
retinoscopy to measure and quantify refractive errors and
diagnose amblyopia. The eye movement, position, and presence of strabismus are done with each evaluation. Indirect
ophthalmoscopy is used to examine the optic nerve, retina,
and retinal vasculature. External eye examination and photographs are done to assess proptosis, enophthalmus, orbital

images of the optic nerve and retina.

Anthropometric Evaluation
All of the patients undergo extensive cranial anthropometric evaluation and analysis using OrthoAmerica’s StarScanner. This device scans the patient’s head using nonionizing,
infrared technology to produce 2-D and 3-D scans with a
large number of data points (Figs. 4.1 and 4.2). A baseline
preoperative scan is done a day or two prior to surgery. On
the fourth postoperative day, the patient is scanned and data
is obtained and sent to the OrthoAmerica’s Orlando manufacturing plant for design, creation, and primary modification of the cranial orthosis. The orthosis is then delivered
overnight and the final modifications are locally made prior
to final fitting. I personally assess the patient and orthosis
for a final check. If further modifications are deemed necessary, they are made prior to instituting an ongoing wear
schedule. Once the helmet is outgrown (usually 8 to 10
weeks for young infants under 3 months of age), the process

is repeated until the conclusion of the orthotic therapy several months later. Specific details on orthosis manufacturing
and management will be given in Chapter 15.

Surgical Set-Up
For all cases, the patient is positioned 180 degrees from the
anesthesiologist. All of the supportive instrumentation is set
to the left of the patient to include the monopolar, bipolar,
and suction units. The endoscopic tower with camera, light,
and cables is also located on the left, as well as the drills.
The surgeon and assistant are situated at the head of the
table and the surgical technician and the instrument and
back tables are located to the right of the patient. Once
sterile draping is done, the instrument pouch on the right
holds the suction, monopolar, and suction coagulator. The
instrument pouch on the left of the patient’s head holds the
second suction bipolar and endoscopic sponge, with Fred
antifog solution (McKesson, Richmond, VA). Two Mayo
stands are situated over the patient’s feet. The Mayo on the
left holds the heavier instruments: endoscopes, drills, and
electric rasp. The Mayo stand on the right holds all of the
instruments used to do the procedure (Fig. 4.3). A towel

Surgical Instrumentation
The overall instrumentation/surgical set up for these cases
is relatively simple and straightforward. The majority of
the instruments are found in most operating rooms and
surgical supply rooms (Fig. 4.4). These include monopolar
electrocautery units utilizing a needle tip, which is set at
15 watts on the cut mode and 20 watts for the coagulation

• Fig. 4.4 Photograph showing the relatively simple set up of the instru-

ments needed to do endoscopic craniosynostosis on a Mayo stand. A
rhinoplasty lighted retractor, endoscopic bipolar, Leksell, Kerrison, and
bone cutting rongeurs, osteotomies, curette, insulated brain ribbons,
forceps, and bone cutting scissors are visualized.

mode. The needle tip on the cut mode is utilized to incise
the dermis followed by the coagulation mode, which is
used for deeper subdermal tissue dissection in a bloodless
fashion. Besides the scalp, the skull and particularly the
diploë are the primary sources of bleeding, given their
vascularity. As such, control of bone bleeding is of utmost
importance to minimize overall blood loss intra- and postoperatively. This bleeding is easily controlled with the use
of a suction coagulator (Fig. 4.5) (Valleylab, Valley Forge,
PA). This unit is connected to the bovie monopolar unit

• Fig. 4.7 Epidural plane dissection with a 30-degree endoscope

• Fig. 4.6 Hopkins rigid, rod-lens endoscopes provide the best resolu-

(ValleyLab) and set at 60 watts on the coagulation mode.
The insulated stem has an uninsulated ring tip, which
delivers the coagulating energy, whilst the inner suction tube continuously aspirates blood to keep the surgical field dry. The suction coagulator is applied to the
bleeding bone until the bleeding stops and the bone fully
coagulated. This maneuver leaves the bone edges black
and charred. As new bone growth (which is membranous
in nature) comes from the dura matter, the bone coagulation does not interfere with new bone growth. There has

not been a single case of bone infection or osteomyelitis
in our case series of over 800 cases. We strongly believe
that this level of osseous coagulation during surgery is the
primary reason for our lack of postoperative bleeding and
the lack of postoperative hematomas or need for reoperations to remove such hematomas. Coupled with the
generous use of Surgiflo (Ethicon, New Brunswick, NJ)
and Thrombin Topical liquid, bleeding from the surgical site is minimized. The use of coagulation on the dura
is kept to an absolute minimum, as its use can lead to
postoperative cranial defects and lack of proper ossification. Every effort must be made to minimize damage to
the osteoprogenitor dural cells during hemostasis. Whenever dural bleeding is encountered, Surgiflo can be used
to stop the bleeding in an atraumatic fashion along with
liquid Thrombin and Gelfoam (Pharmacia and Upjohn,
New York, NY).

Endoscopes
Standard 4-mm rigid, rod-lens endoscopes are used to
visualize the subgaleal and epidural spaces during dissection (Fig. 4.6). The zero-degree and the 30-degree rigid
endoscopes provide excellent visualization and illumination (Karl Storz, Germany). The 30-degree endoscope is
used to illuminate and visualize the undersurface of the
bone (Fig. 4.7) when developing the epidural space dissection. The zero-degree endoscope is used for all other visualization and for endoscopic bone coagulation after the
osteotomies are performed (Fig. 4.8). Flexible endoscopes
are not used as they provide lower resolution and illumination and are difficult to handle along with the other dissecting instruments.

CHAPTER 4

Perioperative Logistics

55

Lighted Retractor

Bone Cutting Scissors/Tools

A very useful instrument when developing the plane of dissection between the galeal and the pericranium is the lighted
retractor (Fig. 4.9). This instrument was originally designed
to be used in rhinoplasty surgery. The blade allows for elevation of the scalp/galea and provides excellent to superb illumination for developing the loose areolar tissue plane (Fig.
4.10). The Aufricht retractor is readily available from any
surgical supply vendor. There are many other variations, but
the underlying principle is a long (13 cm) and wide (1 cm)
blade with a built-in fiber optic light which readily illuminates the surgical field.

Creation of osteotomies and resection of stenosed sutures
underlie the basic and primary tenet of treating infants with
craniosynostosis using minimally invasive endoscopic techniques. As such, the tools used to make these bony cuts vary
depending on the age of the patient and the thickness of the
bone. For the most part and for the majority of the patients,
a sharp set of standard curved Mayo scissors usually suffice. The younger the patient (under 3 months), the thinner
the cranial vault and the easier it is to cut the bone. As the
patient gets older (3 to 6 months), the bone thickens and
the diploë becomes more robust. The Mayo scissors can easily be used in children up to 6 months of age with little difficulty. For older patients or younger patients with unusually
wide and firm bone, a special bone-cutting scissors has been
developed and designed with the Storz company (Karl Storz
SE & CD. Kq, Tuttingen, Germany) (Fig. 4.11). The design
of the scissors allows the cutting blades to easily cut through
thick, hard, dense bone with minimal resistance (Fig. 4.12).
We reserve the use of these scissors for those older patients
with much denser and wider bone. Other instruments can
be used to cut the bone and these include the Piezosurgery
Plus (Mectron S.P.A., Carasco, Italy) with bone cutting
blades which minimize injury to the neovascular tissue and
dura. This device is excellent for making relatively bloodless
and very precise osteotomies in older patients (Fig. 4.13).
Some surgeons have successfully used the Sonopet (Stryker,
Kalamazoo, MI) ultrasonic aspirator to cut the bone. We do
not have experience with this device.

Specialized Instrumentation

• Fig. 4.11 Bone cutting scissors designed for endoscopic craniosyn-

Our team worked with the Storz company and developed
several important sets of instruments specifically designed
to assist with these surgeries. Of particular importance is
the J&B (Jimenez/Barone) dural retractor (Fig. 4.14). This
device is designed to insert and hold a zero-degree endoscope in between a pair of movable and interchangeable
retracting blades. As previously stated, osseous coagulation
is very important to minimize bleeding and the need for
blood transfusions. The J&B dural retractor is invaluable
during bone coagulation. Designed for a right-handed surgeon, it is held in the left hand. Its design allows the introduction of the suction-coagulator with the right hand (Fig.
4.15).
The upper retracting blade elevates and protects the scalp
and galea. The lower retracting blade depresses and protects
the dura. The zero-degree endoscope is locked in place halfway along the length of the blades and then the entire unit
moves in unison, allowing the surgeon to protect the soft
tissues while coagulating the bone safely (Fig. 4.16). When
used in metopic or coronal synostosis, with smaller osteotomies and openings, the narrower blades can be successfully
used. When removing bone and developing the osteotomies, another useful group of instruments include the bone
cutting rongeurs. There are several types but, in general,
these instruments have long shafts and cutting cups, as seen
in Fig. 4.17. The ones shown are the Ferris-Smith rongeurs
(CMF Medicon Surgical). They come in different configurations depending on the angle of bone removal needed. The
bone is removed in a piecemeal fashion (Fig. 4.18). These
instruments are particularly useful and necessary when
removing the frontal bone near the nasofrontal suture when

ostosis surgery consist of a long lever handle arms and short cutting
blades, which allows for easily cutting bone of different thicknesses.

create paramedian osteotomies in a 7-month-old male with sagittal
synostosis.

treating metopic craniosynostosis. They are also useful when
removing bone near the pterion and squamosal suture when
treating coronal craniosynostosis.

the bipolar unit is such that it may be introduced in very

Conclusion
As with any other surgical procedure, proper planning and
execution is extremely important for a successful outcome
with minimal or no complications. The proper instrumentation is also necessary to perform a satisfactory surgery. This
chapter outlined the preoperative steps that our team takes
prior to surgery and the key instrumentation needed for the
surgery. We have been very successful with our surgeries and
outcomes using the aforementioned protocols and instruments. Each chapter in the book will describe the technique
in detail for each specific surgery.

The Role of the Nurse in
Endoscopic Craniosynostosis
Surgery
CATHY C. CARTWRIGHT, DNP, RN-BC, PCNS, FAAN

Introduction
The role of the nurse in endoscopic craniosynostosis surgery began in 1996, after the first surgery was performed
at the University of Missouri-Columbia.1 The role involved
not only patient care and family communication, but also
coordinating a new type of program. Many of the lessons
learned during the early years of program development are
still applicable today. The role of the Advanced Practice Registered Nurse (APRN) is particularly suited for care coordination, pre- and postoperative teaching, follow-up care,
nursing staff education, data collection, and research. The
nurse should be knowledgeable about infant skull anatomy
and the physiology of skull bone and brain growth, as it
is essential in explaining the minimally invasive endoscopic
treatment of craniosynostosis. Prompt, accurate communication with the parents is essential to decrease their
anxiety. Parents report that step-by-step information about
what to expect from diagnosis to discharge and follow-up
is extremely helpful. Perhaps most important, listening and
addressing parents’ concerns, answering their questions, and
supporting their decisions provide the greatest support to
the parents at such a difficult time for them.

Making the Diagnosis
Craniosynostosis occurs in approximately 1 out of 2500
live births.2 As the brain grows rapidly during the first year
of life, the skull bones are normally pushed apart and new
bone is formed perpendicular at the suture sites.3 Genetic
mutations cannot be found in 85% of the infants with
craniosynostosis and those are called “nonsyndromic.”4 A
stenosed (closed) suture prevents skull growth (perpendicular) to the suture and results in the deformities seen in
an infant with craniosynostosis.5 Parents may notice some
molding of the skull at birth due to childbirth, but the
shape usually normalizes by 3 months of age6 or sooner.
Although the diagnosis of craniosynostosis is usually made

by physical examination, it is often apparent in photographs
furnished by parents. Skull radiographs or a computed axial
tomography (CT) scan can be ordered to confirm a questionable diagnosis. Further imaging, such as a magnetic
resonance imaging (MRI), can be done if there is a concern
for hydrocephalus or other brain anomalies. Care must be
taken when considering radiodiagnostic imaging as ionizing radiation can have a negative effect on a growing child’s
brain. The Joint Commission (2011) recommends using
ALARA guidelines, meaning that the dose should be “as low
as reasonably achievable” to minimize exposure to ionizing
radiation.7
Parents frequently recognize there is something wrong
with their baby’s head at birth, or within a few weeks afterward. It is extremely important to listen to parents’ concerns about their infant’s head shape so the diagnosis can be
made early enough that they can have a choice of treatment
options. Some parents have reported they noticed that the
eyes were not leveled, or the nose was twisted, or they felt
a ridge on top of their baby’s head, but their concerns were
dismissed by their healthcare provider. Unfortunately, not
all healthcare providers are trained to recognize craniosynostosis. Oftentimes providers adopt the “watch and wait”
attitude which can delay the diagnosis of craniosynostosis
past 6 months of age, thereby practically eliminating the
window of opportunity for the minimally invasive option
of endoscopic surgery. This can present a dilemma for parents who suspect that there is something wrong with their
infant’s head soon after birth. In reviewing the many craniosynostosis websites and parental blogs, the anger and
frustration of parents whose concerns were put off until
their baby was too old for the minimally invasive endoscopy-assisted procedure can be seen. Some parents express
feelings of guilt for not insisting on a referral to a craniofacial center earlier. Best results are obtained when the endoscopic surgery is performed by age 3 months and typically
no later than 6 months, followed by strict use of a custommade cranial molding helmet. This emphasizes the need
59

booksmedicos.org

60 C HA P T ER 5

The Role of the Nurse in Endoscopic Craniosynostosis Surgery

for early diagnosis. Parents must also be made aware of the
availability of the endoscopic alternative, so the surgery can
be done before the infant becomes too old for it to be effective. Although the authors of this book are proponents of
the endoscopic technique, some neurosurgeons and plastic surgeons advocate the open procedures, and there are
centers where the minimally invasive technique is not performed. If done correctly and with appropriate follow-up,
both types of surgery (endoscopic and open) will produce
good results. There are many advantages, however, to the
endoscopic technique, which have been discussed throughout this book. Hence, parents must be given accurate information so they can decide what is the best treatment for
their child.
Although nonsyndromic craniosynostosis typically
involves only one suture and is not associated with other
anomalies seen with syndromic craniosynostosis, the
nurse should be aware that receiving the diagnosis of
craniosynostosis can have a profound effect on families.8
Being told your infant needs surgery is frightening for
parents in general, but surgery on the skull or face seems
to evoke a particular level of anxiety and fear. Parents
are faced with making the choice of surgery to change
their child’s facial appearance or choosing no surgery with
questionable risks of damage to neurocognitive development.4 It is important to listen to their concerns and provide them with evidence-based information, so they can
make an informed decision. Many parents struggle with
whether to submit their infant to surgery for “cosmetic”
reasons, stating they would love their child no matter how
he or she looked. However, there can be psychological and
physical implications if nothing is done. Children who
look different than their peers are often teased or bullied, which can negatively affect their self-esteem. Hats
and sports helmets may not fit appropriately due to the
abnormal shape, further adding to the feeling of being
different from their peers.
Parents may have concerns about cognitive development
as well as cosmesis, as some studies have suggested there
is cognitive impairment and neurodevelopmental delay
related to single suture craniosynostosis.9 Many centers recommend neurodevelopmental screening for patients with
single suture craniosynostosis.10 In a qualitative study of
parents of infants with single suture, nonsyndromic craniosynostosis, Kuta8 found that parents appreciated the support of healthcare professionals, especially having access
to a specialized nurse to ask questions of during the process. Talking with other families who have been through
a similar experience was found to be particularly valuable.
Information from the craniofacial team is helpful, but parents often want to hear the perspective of others who have
been through the entire process. Most parents of infants
who have undergone endoscopic craniosynostosis surgery
are very willing to share their experience with other families. Obtain written permission according to hospital policy
when connecting families whose children have the same
types of craniosynostosis.

Websites
Websites can be an important tool in educating parents and
other healthcare providers. There are numerous websites
that reference the endoscopic-assisted procedure, including
those sponsored by hospitals, surgeons, or parents. Unfortunately, some of them contain inaccurate or misleading
information, and may even claim that the procedure does
not work, or that patients will have to undergo further
reconstructive surgery in the future. The nurse should be
prepared to answer questions arising from such misinformation. Reviewing the website of the craniofacial team planning to do the surgery yields the most accurate information
on outcomes from that particular center. Parents may select
a center based largely on its website, so the information
should be regularly updated.
The APRN should be a content expert for the craniosynostosis website and collaborate with the institution’s
website designer or communication/marketing department. It is essential the website accurately describes the
various types of craniosynostosis and treatment options.
Pre- and postoperative photographs of each type of synostosis help parents understand the anomaly and outcomes
from surgery. Parent testimonials can provide relatable
information for parents considering craniosynostosis
surgery. Emphasis must be placed on the importance of
seeing the infant in craniosynostosis clinic as soon as possible to give parents the option of the endoscopic surgery.
For the purpose of consistency, it is generally preferable
for the APRN who coordinates the program to respond
to parent inquiries. Including a contact number or email
address on the website and responding promptly to queries
is extremely gratifying to anxious parents seeking information about surgical options.

The Endoscopic-Assisted Procedure
Substantial information about the endoscopic-assisted procedure is available throughout the other chapters of this
book. However, the nurse should be able to provide an
explanation of the endoscopic surgery and reasons for postoperative cranial molding therapy to parents in terms they
can understand.
Infants generally experience rapid head growth during the first year of life, and especially during the first few
months, as the brain expands. The head circumference
increases 9 cm during the first 6 months after birth and only
3 cm from 6 months to 1 year of age. The cranial sutures
remain open to accommodate this expansion, but if a suture
is closed at birth or closes prematurely, then a deformity
will likely result. Using small incisions to minimize trauma
and blood loss, the surgeon removes a strip of bone containing the stenosed suture, which then allows the brain to
expand normally. The endoscope allows the surgeon direct
visualization of the bone, craniectomy site, dura, and any
bleeding that occurs. The intact dura begins forming new
bone at the craniectomy site, a process that occurs rapidly

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 5

in a young infant, and diminishes during the first year of
life. Early surgical intervention takes advantage of this rapid
bone growth. New bone is formed as “islands” that gradually enlarge to cover the dura and close the cranial defect.
This is most apparent in infants with sagittal synostosis who
have had a wide vertex craniectomy. Palpating the top of the
head immediately after surgery feels like palpating a large
anterior fontanel. However, over the next several weeks or
months, the small islands of bone can be felt, enlarging until
their edges meet forming solid bone. This process should be
explained to parents, so they will know what to expect as the
defect closes. Several days after surgery, the infant is placed
in a custom-made molding helmet to redirect the growth of
the skull as new bone is formed, so that the head is properly
shaped as the natural growth process occurs. The helmet is
typically worn for 6 to 12 months, depending on the infant’s
age at the time of surgery and type of suture affected, and
periodically replaced as the head changes shape. Rigorous
follow-up with access to a competent orthotist familiar with
helmet therapy for postoperative endoscopic craniosynostosis surgery is key to successful outcomes. The importance
of wearing the helmet as directed should be stressed to the
parents.

Preoperative Preparation
In many instances the nurse is the only healthcare provider
from the craniofacial team that the parents communicate
with before the first clinic visit. Much of the preoperative
patient history and teaching can be done virtually through
telehealth or by phone. It also gives the nurse the opportunity to answer questions and correct any misconceptions
parents may have read on the internet or heard from wellintentioned friends and relatives. Describing what to expect
during preoperative preparation, surgery, postoperative
recovery, discharge home and follow-up care is extremely
important to parents and increases their comfort level.8 It is
important to remember that the parents may be quite emotional due to anxiety over the possibility of impending surgery and relieved that a minimally invasive technique is an
option. The nurse orchestrates the entire process from the
first communication with the family, through preoperative
preparation, postoperative care, discharge, and follow-up
appointments.
The nurse should be prepared to answer the more commonly asked questions such as:
• How long has the surgeon been doing this type of endoscopic surgery?
• Have any patients needed more surgery at a later date?
• Which type of surgery would you choose, the endoscopic
or open procedure?
• Would you let this surgeon operate on your infant?
• Have any patients died?
• What complications have occurred?
• Will the baby be in much pain?
• How long does the surgery last?
• Will my future babies have this condition also?

The Role of the Nurse in Endoscopic Craniosynostosis Surgery

61

Preoperative Clinic Visit
The importance of collecting accurate data is underscored
to provide objective evaluation of outcomes. Surgeons, parents, and nurses could have unconscious bias when assessing postoperative results. Parents may tend to see good
outcomes to validate their decision to submit their infant
for surgery, whereas the surgeon may tend to see a good
outcome as proof of surgical skill. Data such as number
of cases, blood loss, postoperative hematocrit, number of
blood transfusions, surgical time, and length of hospital stay
can be used to demonstrate outcomes. Parents appreciate
the transparency when those data are shared with them. Preand postoperative anthropomorphic measurements and
photographs are essential in objectively evaluating outcomes
from surgery and helmet therapy. Orthotists use modern
scanning technology to obtain baseline cranial measurements and a 3-D picture of the skull prior to surgery. The
nurse can use cranial calipers to obtain the cephalic index
(CI) of infants with sagittal synostosis by measuring euryon
to euryon and glabella to opisthocranion. Dividing the
euryon number by the opisthocranion number and multiplying by 100 provides the cephalic index. The lower the
number, the longer (more scaphocephalic) the head. The
CI will increase after surgery as the head becomes rounder.
Normal CI is between 75 and 85 (Fig. 5.1). Photographs
are another way to objectively measure changes as the skull
reshapes over time. Parents sign consent for photographs at
the first preoperative clinic appointment. Several views of
the skull and face are photographed: vertex (bird’s eye view
of the top of the skull), lateral (each side of the skull), frontal (full face), and posterior. Particular attention is given to
views of the forehead to show frontal bossing, asymmetry,
or flattening of the superior orbital rim. A drape provides
a neutral background for each view. Rarely will an infant
remain motionless for photos, so it is helpful to have the
parent, child life specialist, or sibling to provide distraction
with a toy or interesting object.
The preoperative history should include perinatal and
birth history, development, coexisting conditions, allergies,
medications, and family medical history, including any
family members who have a misshapen head or a diagnosis
of craniosynostosis. Any history of excessive bleeding from
the patient (e.g., circumcision) or family member should
be investigated. The physical assessment should include a
thorough neurological examination with particular attention to the skull and head circumference measurement.
The head should be viewed from the vertex, lateral, anterior, and posterior positions, noting any asymmetry and
palpating for ridges along suture lines. Infants with sagittal synostosis characteristically have a scaphocephalic or
boat-shaped appearance to the head with a palpable sagittal
ridge, frontal bossing, bitemporal narrowing, and occipital
cupping. In some cases, the occipital cupping is so pronounced that the infant is unable to lie with the occiput
on a surface as the degree of cervical flexion can compromise the airway. Sagittal suture synostosis is found in 40%

to 60% of children with nonsyndromic craniosynostosis
(Fig. 5.2).11 Unilateral coronal craniosynostosis occurs in
20% to 35% of children with craniosynostosis (Fig. 5.3).10
Infants with unilateral coronal synostosis (anterior plagiocephaly) typically have nasional deviation to the ipsilateral
(affected) side, vertical dystopia, flattening of the frontal
bone on the ipsilateral side with compensatory bossing
of the frontal bone on the contralateral side. Paresis of

the ipsilateral superior oblique can cause strabismus and
a compensatory head tilt. It is recommended that these
infants be evaluated preoperatively by an ophthalmologist
familiar with craniofacial disorders. Bicoronal synostosis,
seen most often in syndromic craniosynostosis, is manifested by brachycephaly, turricephaly, and flattening of the
frontal bone. These patients should be referred to genetics
for workup of genetic mutations.

CHAPTER 5

Metopic suture synostosis was traditionally thought to
occur in 10% to 15% of children with craniosynostosis,
but recent studies have found increased incidences, some
as high as 420% (Fig. 5.4).11 It is unknown whether these
increases are attributable to better diagnostic techniques,
the inclusion of children with minor metopic ridges in the
statistics, or some yet unexplained environmental factor.
Infants with metopic synostosis have a triangular appearance
(trigonocephaly) to their head with bitemporal narrowing,

• Fig. 5.3 Top view of a 3-month-old male with right coronal synosto-

sis. Seen are right frontal plagiocephaly, proptosis, nasal deviation, and
left frontal bossing.

A

The Role of the Nurse in Endoscopic Craniosynostosis Surgery

63

hypotelorism (close-set eyes), parietal bossing, and a metopic
ridge. Weinzweig12 found that metopic suture was typically
fused by 8 months of age in infants without synostosis and
early fusion occurred at 3 months of age. However, many
infants may display a slight metopic ridge without any other
characteristics of metopic synostosis. This ridge usually disappears as the frontal bones thicken and the skull grows
over the next 2 to 3 years.13 Surgery is not considered for
these infants unless there is undue fussiness and irritability.
Lambdoid synostosis (positional plagiocephaly) is uncommon (1%–5%)14 and must not be confused with positional
plagiocephaly. An infant with lambdoid synostosis has trapezoid shape to the head when viewed from the top and a
tilted skull base with the affected side displaced inferiorly
when viewed posteriorly. The more common positional plagiocephaly is caused by the effect of gravitational forces on
the skull as the head lies on a firm surface such as a mattress.
Infants with positional plagiocephaly have a parallelogram
shape to their head when viewed from the top and the skull
base is horizontal when viewed posteriorly.
Preoperative appointments with the craniofacial team,
anesthesia, and orthotics should be coordinated to minimize the time parents must take off work or travel. The
nurse often plays the role of “concierge” when arranging
appointments and consults, especially for families traveling from out of town who are not familiar with the city.
Although blood transfusions are not usually needed for the
minimally invasive endoscopic craniosynostosis surgery, a
type and screen may be ordered so blood can be available
if a transfusion is needed. If parents prefer to donate blood
prior to surgery, it must be arranged through the hospital’s blood bank, which may take several days to process.
Insurance may not cover the cost of donation. Preoperative

B

• Fig. 5.4 (A) Direct top view of 7-week-old male with metopic synostosis. (B) Modified top view of same
patient shows marked forehead recession bilaterally, mid frontal prominence, and ridging.

The Role of the Nurse in Endoscopic Craniosynostosis Surgery

bloodwork for hematocrit and type and screen can be drawn
in the operating room after the infant is asleep to minimize
trauma to the child and the parents. Some parents (Jehovah’s
Witnesses) prefer their infant not receive a blood transfusion for religious reasons and thereby choose the minimally
invasive surgical option instead of the open procedure.
Parents should discuss this preference with the surgeon at
the initial visit so a plan can be made if the infant were to
require a blood transfusion.

Insurance
It is important for third-party payers to note the difference
between cosmetic and reconstructive surgery when considering eligibility for covered services. Endoscopic surgery
to correct craniosynostosis is reconstructive, not cosmetic
surgery. The American Medical Association states cosmetic
surgery is “surgery performed to reshape normal structures
of the body in order to improve the patient’s appearance and
self-esteem. Reconstructive surgery is performed on abnormal structures of the body, caused by congenital defects,
developmental abnormalities, trauma, infection, tumors, or
disease. It is generally performed to improve function but
may also be done to approximate a normal appearance.”15

Postoperative Care
At the completion of surgery, a postoperative hematocrit is
drawn in the operating room or recovery room and again
before discharge. Most infants will proceed to the general
care floor and do not need an intensive care stay unless they
have a coexisting condition. Elevating the head 30 degrees
helps to minimize facial swelling. Rarely does periorbital
swelling interfere with eye opening. Position infants with
sagittal synostosis with the back of the head on the mattress
so gravity can assist with decreasing the anterior-posterior
(AP) distance postcraniectomy. Advise parents that the head
may feel “boggy” the first day or two after surgery due to
swelling from tissue manipulation, but this is expected. The
swelling will be most pronounced on the most dependent
side of the head. If a subgaleal drain was placed (uncommonly) in the operating room to decrease postoperative
swelling, it is generally discontinued the following day.
There is usually no dressing, although the incision may be
closed with Mastisol and Steri-strips. Many parents note an
improvement in head shape immediately postoperatively,
particularly in infants with sagittal synostosis. Preoperative
diet and activity can be resumed on the same day as surgery,
and parents should be allowed to handle their baby as they
usually would. Nursing care includes careful monitoring of
vital signs, neurological status, and observation for signs of
bleeding. Acetaminophen and ibuprofen can be given for
pain with fentanyl or morphine as needed for breakthrough
pain. Most infants are fussy for the first 8 hours and then
begin to behave at their baseline. Some parents of previously
fussy infants report an immediate and sustained decrease in
fussiness after surgery. The parents should be encouraged

to hold their infant and provide familiar comfort measures
before offering narcotics as most babies are hungry and want
to be fed. Care must be taken to prevent over sedation that
can prevent the infant from eating, thus prolonging hospital
stay. The infant can be discharged the next day with a stable
hematocrit, adequate oral intake, and pain is controlled
with oral medication. Instruct parents to call for fussiness
not relieved by pain medication, vomiting, lethargy, or any
drainage from the incision site. They should wash the incision daily with baby shampoo but do not soak the scalp. The
orthotist will measure the infant’s head for a custom-made
molding helmet 2 to 3 days after surgery when swelling
decreases. Parents may be concerned about the potential for
injury to the brain during that time, as there is no bone covering the defect from the craniectomy site and no helmet for
protection. Reassure them that no extra precautions need
to be taken as the dura is quite tough (dura mater = “hard
mother”). They should protect the infant’s head just as they
normally would. The importance of wearing the helmet as
prescribed for best results, from 6 months to 1+ year should
be emphasized. Less than optimum results can occur when
the helmet is not worn properly or discontinued too soon.
The purpose of the helmet is to reshape the skull as it
grows, overcoming the dural forces dictating skull growth
that caused the deformity. The orthotist will follow the head
growth and helmet fit over the next several months, adjusting or constructing a new helmet when necessary. Infants
generally tolerate wearing the helmet, and parents adorn
them with the child’s name, sports team logos, stickers, and
other decorations. Several parents have remarked that they
have been approached by other parents asking how they can
get a similar helmet for their child, perhaps seeing it as a
fashion accessory (Fig. 5.5). One father of a “helmet baby”
described how his son only watched TV when a football
game was broadcast, as if he was fascinated by the helmets
the players wore. The infant should follow up in craniofacial clinic 2 weeks after surgery and at scheduled intervals
during helmet therapy. Timely communication between
the nurse and orthotist can improve patient outcomes. For
example, the orthotist may see the patient more frequently
than the nurse or craniofacial team and would report any
problems with the incision, or abnormal head growth. The
nurse would alert the orthotist to excess redness on pressure points from the helmet so an adjustment can be made.
The head circumference is checked at each visit and the CI
is measured for patients with sagittal synostosis. Although
rare, hydrocephalus can occur and affect the growth of the
skull, altering the mechanics of helmet therapy. The incision
should be dry and without redness or drainage. Although
most sutures are absorbable, remove any that remain. Photographs should be taken at each visit to document progressive reshaping.

Conclusion
Receiving the diagnosis of craniosynostosis and choosing
surgical correction is extremely stressful for parents as they

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 5

A

The Role of the Nurse in Endoscopic Craniosynostosis Surgery

B

65

C

• Fig. 5.5 (A–C) Helmets are commonly painted or wrapped by parents providing unique patterns and
improving the orthosis’ aesthetics.

embark on the journey to correct their infant’s head shape. The
nurse is instrumental in guiding parents through all aspects
of this experience by providing prompt, accurate information and answering their questions about what to expect.
Parents are generally relieved when the surgery is over and
happily surprised that their infant did so well. Many become
advocates for the minimally invasive procedure and seek to
inform the medical community and other parents about
endoscopic craniosynostosis surgery and the importance of
early diagnosis. It is recommended that children with single
suture synostosis be followed by a multidisciplinary craniofacial team until at least 6 years of age due to their risk for
neurodevelopmental problems.10,16 Patient follow-up over
the years not only provides additional evidence regarding the
effectiveness of the endoscopic-assisted technique, but also
reinforces the job satisfaction and joy nurses experience in
helping patients and families along this journey.

Anesthesia Management
DAVID F. JIMENEZ, MD, FACS, MARC A. ORLANDI, MD,
AND TINA M. WHITMAN, DNP, CRNA

Introduction
Craniosynostosis surgery presents a unique set of challenges
to the anesthesiology team caring for the patient, particularly
when surgery is done on young infants. Amongst a number
of potential problems and concerns, the two most prominent
are extensive blood losses and venous air emboli (VAE). As
each child is different and presents with unique characteristics
and concerns, the preoperative assessment needs to be tailored
to each individual patient. Airway evaluation and appraisal is
essential, particularly in cases where craniofacial syndromes
are present. The same applies to cardiac assessment in association with syndromes. Historically, treatment has evolved and
nowadays, most craniofacial programs in large children’s hospitals perform the type of procedures commonly known as
calvarial vault remodeling (CVR). These operations are extensive, invasive, traumatic in nature, and typically associated
with large blood losses. For this type of procedure, baseline
blood work needs to be collected preoperatively and should
include hematological values, electrolytes, and coagulation
studies. Given the concern for possible sudden large volume
blood loss, blood products should be ordered and be available
at time of induction. Craniosynostosis vault surgery is perhaps one of the only surgeries where the anesthesiology team
gives the patient blood products prior to skin incision. This
is particularly the case when the patient presents with low
blood volumes and values. Careful monitoring of the patient
during the surgery requires placement and insertion of arterial peripheral lines, a central venous line, multiple large bore
intravenous lines, and Foley catheter.
With the introduction of minimally invasive endoscopicassisted techniques in 1998,1 the overall anesthetic management of patients afflicted with craniosynostosis has evolved
significantly. In this chapter we will present our team’s
approach to the successful application of anesthetic management principles to minimally invasive craniosynostosis
techniques.

Preoperative Evaluation and Assessment
A careful history is imperative to ascertain possible intraoperative risks and complications as well as to properly

prepare for unexpected events. Salient points to consider
include preterm versus term birth, congenital cardiac/pulmonary defects, history of increased intracranial pressure,
and/or any other factors that required admission to the
neonatal intensive care unit (NICU). The physical examination should be focused on the airway assessment, breath
sounds, and cardiac examination. Patients with a history
of heart murmur should have a cardiology consult and an
echocardiogram performed prior to surgery. A final critical
consideration is the physiologic anemia of the newborn. At
the nadir of anemia, in full-term infants, hemoglobin levels may be as low as 9 to 10 g/100 mL at 6 weeks of age.2
Given the short anesthetic time and minimal blood losses
associated with endoscopic craniosynostosis procedures, we
do not routinely obtain extensive preoperative blood work
but simply rely on a hemoglobin/hematocrit (H/H) level
and a type and screen, which is obtained after the patient is
induced under general anesthesia. We do not type and cross
our patients as the need of a blood transfusion (postoperatively) has been very rare during the last 10 years.

Positioning Issues
Depending on the type of suture involved, the patient positioning will vary. In all circumstances, following intubation,
the table will be rotated 180 degrees away from the anesthesia provider. This requires a heightened level of attention
on the part of the anesthesia team. The length of the circuit
is increased and there are more potential opportunities for
circuit disconnect (Fig. 6.1). For correction of metopic and
coronal craniosynostosis, the patients are placed in neutral
position, supine, the head resting on a cerebellar horseshoe
headrest. The stability of this positioning affords a lessened
chance for endotracheal (ET) tube movement or dislodgement. Patients presenting with lambdoid synostosis can be
placed with head turned fully to the contralateral side resting
on a cerebellar horseshoe headrest or can be placed on the
lateral decubitus position, with the head also on a cerebellar
headrest. Patients presenting with bilateral craniosynostosis
are placed prone on a cerebellar headrest. Care must be taken
that the eyes are fully protected and free of pressure (Fig. 6.2).
The most challenging of all positions in the modified prone

Preoperative Preparation

• Fig. 6.2 Placing the patient fully prone also increases the risk of tube

(sphinx) position that is used to treat sagittal craniosynostosis. The sphinx mandates prone positioning with the neck in

of the ET tube in a cephalad direction. For this reason, it is
recommended that the ET tube be placed 1 to 2 cm past the

Careful planning and preparation are necessary, given the fact
that a large number of these patients are between 6 and 24
weeks old. NPO (nil per OS) placement must be appropriately timed to prevent dehydration. Our protocol includes
no oral intake of clear liquids 2 hours prior to induction, 4
hours for patients nursing and on breast milk, and 6 hours
for patients on commercial formula. Generally, premedication
is not required. As with all other surgeries in infants, a fever,
lower respiratory tract finding on examination such as wheezes
or crackles are appropriate reasons for postponing surgery until
the patient has been symptom free for 2 weeks. A patient with
a developing upper respiratory infection has a 5- to10-times
greater risk of developing bronchospasm and laryngospasm.

Anesthetic Management
For a successful case with minimal complications, there
should be a number of preparatory steps prior to induction.

Anesthesia Management

• Fig. 6.4 To minimize endotracheal tube dislodgement or movement,
a throat pack should be placed (red arrow). The tube tape should
extend close to the ear and across the entire cheek bilaterally (green
arrow), and Tegaderm with Mastisol placed over it (yellow arrow).

• Fig. 6.5 All pressure points (white circles) should be properly pad-

ded with soft cast padding in order to prevent unwanted skin injury or
pressure ulcers.

The surgical suite should be prewarmed appropriately to
minimize hypothermia and its negative effects. The use of a
radiant heat lamp is common. A pediatric heated breathing
circuit (or humidivent) should be used. A warm air blanket should be set up and used throughout the entire case.
A pediatric intravenous (IV) set up using a BURETROL
(Baxter, Mississauga, Ontario, Canada) with extra extension
is set up (Fig. 6.6). Isotonic IV solutions are used, taking

• Fig. 6.6 The Buretrol infusion device holds limited quantities of intra-

venous fluids and it is designed to prevent rapid and free flow of fluids
as infants are less likely to handle large and sudden influx of fluids
during surgery.

care to avoid and remove any air bubbles in the line, as 50%
of children under 5 have a patent foramen ovale.
Standard monitoring including EKG, pulse oximetry,
and noninvasive blood pressure monitoring is set up preinduction. Central temperature monitoring (nasal, esophageal, or rectal) is used and not skin temperature as the latter
is unreliable. An inhalational induction with sevoflurane
is performed in all cases unless there are contraindications
to the use of volatile agents. Nitrous oxide is avoided due
to the risk of VAE following induction of general anesthesia. Intravenous line placement is begun with the goal of
obtaining venous access with peripheral lines. There is no
need to place arterial or central venous lines. Muscle relaxation can be obtained with a muscle relaxant of choice
(i.e., Rocuronium, 1 mg/kg). Postinduction, an intravenous antibiotic is given and timed for 30 minutes prior to
skin incision. Cefazolin at 25 or 50 mg/kg may be given.
Tranexamic acid is given prior to starting the procedure to
help minimize bleeding at a dosage of 50 mg/kg. Fluid management includes the use of either 0.9% sodium chloride or
lactated ringer’s solution for a maximum of 10 mL/kg given
throughout the case. Intraoperative analgesia is managed
with a combination of intravenous fentanyl, at a dose of 2
to 3 μg/kg titrated during surgery, along with the administration of Ofirmev at 10 to 15 μg/kg.

Venous Air Embolism
VAE is defined as the entrainment of air from the surgical field
into the venous vasculature, ultimately entering the heart and
leading to the production of systemic effects. The true incidence is unknown, but it has been reported to be as high
as 83% in craniosynostosis surgery.3 If sudden and large
amounts of air are entrapped, the effects of VAE can be
devastating and fatal. The risk of an adverse event occurring
is directly related to patient positioning and the location
of the head in relation to the patient’s heart. Most cases
of craniosynostosis are of the sagittal type and the position used for most sagittal cases is the “sphinx” or modified

prone position. This position places the patient’s top of
the head (sagittal sinus) significantly higher than the heart
(Fig. 6.7). At such height differential, even if a small opening develops over the sagittal sinus or a small venous perforator is open, air can easily be suctioned into the venous
system and lead to clinical problems. Fortunately, most
VAE are subclinical and result in no negative effects to the
patient. The primary factors which determine morbidity
and mortality in VAE are the volume of air entrained and
the rate of entrainment. These variables are directly related
to head position in cases of gravitational gradient driver
VAE, as in the case in these surgeries. In dogs, lethal air
volumes in VAE have been calculated to be 7.5 to 15 mL/
kg.4 The exact amount for humans is not known, but the
best estimate has been made from case reports of accidental intravascular air delivery5 and has been calculated to be
3 to 5 mL/kg. The symptoms associated with VAE include
bronchoconstriction and wheezing, hypotension, circulatory collapse, hypoxemia, dysrhythmia, and myocardial
ischemia. Signs include acute decrease or loss of end-tidal
CO2, increased end-tidal nitrogen, oxygen desaturation,
pulmonary hypertension, right heart strain, ST changes,
peaked P waves, and, ultimately, cardiovascular collapse.
Because of the disastrous nature of undetected VAE, it has
been our policy to carefully monitor all of our patients
using a precordial doppler (Fig. 6.8). Precordial doppler
ultrasound (PDU) is the most sensitive of the noninvasive
monitoring modalities and is capable of detecting as little
as 0.25 mL of air (0.05 mL/kg).6 The PDU probe can be
placed on either side of the sternal border. The goal is to

booksmedicos.org

70 C HA P T ER 6

Anesthesia Management

End of Case Issues
Upon conclusion of surgery, emergence from anesthesia is
performed once the table has been rotated back 180 degrees
to assure optimal airway access. Thereafter, reversal of neuromuscular blockade can be performed, along with the elimination of all inhalational anesthetics. An “awake” emergence
is preferred for transport to the postanesthesia care unit
(PACU). PACU care includes supplemental oxygen, analgesics, repeat hemoglobin and hematocrit, maintenance of
normothermia, and resumption of oral intake. Once discharge criteria have been met, the patient is transferred to
the pediatric intensive care unit for overnight observation
and typical discharge to home the following morning.

Conclusion

• Fig. 6.10 Once consistent doppler signals are obtained, care should
be taken to securely tape the probe in place, which can be easily done
with foam tape.

place the probe along the right heart border so that right
ventricular outflow tract signals can be detected (Figs.
6.9 and 6.10). Turbulence in the blood flow pattern will
be detected with any VAE and can be heard as a sound
of a “washing machine.” We do not use the most sensitive but most invasive detection method, transesophageal
echocardiography, nor do we use or insert central venous
lines. The management of VAE is primarily aimed at stopping the entrapment and aspiration of air. This is done by
the surgeon immediately flooding the surgical field with
fluid irrigation and the concurrent lowering of the head
to or below the level of the heart. Other measure includes
performing a Valsalva maneuver with manual ventilation
and institution of 100% oxygen therapy. Given the short
nature of the endoscopic cases and constant monitoring
of VAE, we have not had any mortalities nor morbidities associated with this phenomenon. No cardiovascular instability nor short- or long-lasting effects have been
encountered. Vigilance is of critical importance to prevent
unwanted complications. Minor VAE sounds are commonly detected, but rapid response by anesthesia and surgical teams has kept us from having significant problems
or complications.

The introduction of minimally invasive endoscopic techniques to the treatment of craniosynostosis has led to simplification and significant improvement in the management
of these patients in the surgical suite. They are associated
with significantly less blood loss with most patients losing
between 10 and 30 mL. No invasive monitoring is required.
Shorter surgical times lead to less heat loss and problems
associated with hypothermia. Smaller incisions and less dissection contribute to less postoperative pain, resulting in
decreased narcotic requirements. In all cases, the anesthesiologist has the ability to safely extubate the patient in the
operating room, with the exception perhaps of an ex-preemie (defined as an infant less than 60 weeks postconceptual
age). These procedures are safer than the traditional calvarial
vault remodeling surgeries.
