Ophthalmic Complications
of Craniosynostosis and
the Impact of Endoscopic
Repair
ABDELRAHMAN M. ELHUSSEINY, MD, AND LINDA R.
DAGI, MD

Introduction
Craniosynostosis often affects the growth and development of the orbit resulting in morphologic changes of the
orbital rim, the trajectory of the orbit, and internal volumetric proportions.1,2 As such, there are a many ways that
vision and ocular alignment are threatened.3–5 Ophthalmic
abnormalities commonly associated with craniosynostosis
include ptosis, exposure keratopathy, astigmatism, amblyopia, papilledema and optic atrophy, and strabismus both
from primary abnormalities of extraocular muscle anatomy
and their trajectory within the orbit, and secondary changes
that can occur after fronto-orbital advancement (FOA).6–8
As such, regular ophthalmic evaluation is essential to prevent vision loss from these multitude of causes, many of
which are treatable. Some centers have reported that 25% to
40% of syndromic craniosynostosis patients will have a best
corrected visual acuity (BCVA) of 20/40 or worse in their
better eye under various protocols of surveillance.9 In this
chapter, we review the ophthalmic complications associated
with craniosynostosis, what is known about their incidence
and severity, and what we have learned when craniosynostosis is treated by endoscopic strip craniectomy (ESC) and
orthosis rather than by FOA.

Ophthalmic Complications of
Craniosynostosis
Refractive Errors and Amblyopia
Several studies have demonstrated that astigmatism is common. Mayer and colleagues have reported that 25% of
healthy children between 1 and 48 months of age have at
Funding: Children’s Hospital Ophthalmology Foundation Chair Funds

least 1 diopter (D) of astigmatism.10 Dobson and colleagues
reported that the incidence of astigmatism (of at least 1 D)
decreases from 50% in children 4.5 to 5.5 years of age to
12% in children 7 to 9 years of age.11 They concluded that
“against-the-rule” astigmatism disappears by school age in
most children.11 Craniosynostosis, whether syndromic or
nonsyndromic, is frequently associated with higher degrees
of oblique astigmatism, anisoastigmatism (significant difference in the amount of astigmatism in one eye compared to
the other), and anisometropia (difference in refractive error
comparing one eye to the other) which increase the risk of
amblyopia.12–16 In a retrospective study evaluating patients
with different forms of craniosynostosis, Hertle and colleagues reported that astigmatism was found in all patients
with Crouzon syndrome (25 patients), 13 of 15 patients
with Apert syndrome, and 9 of 18 patients with other forms
of craniosynostosis. They noted that refractive errors are a
significant cause of vision loss in this population for several
reasons.17 Detection and adequate treatment may be limited if photophobia from exposure keratopathy limits the
ability to perform an accurate refraction, and midface retrusion combined with relative exorbitism or proptosis creates
technical challenges to wearing glasses or other forms of
optical correction.
Nonsyndromic Craniosynostosis
Unilateral Coronal Synostosis (UCS). Denis and colleagues evaluated 39 patients with UCS and reported
anisoastigmatism of at least 1.5 D in 9 patients with equal
frequency in the ipsilateral or contralateral eye.15 In contrast,
Levy and colleagues reported that 54% (21 out of 39) of
patients had at least 1 D of astigmatism, and of these patients,
76% had 1 D or greater astigmatism in the eye contralateral
to the fused coronal suture.12 Only 7 out of 39 patients had
71

booksmedicos.org

72 C HA P T ER 7

Ophthalmic Complications of Craniosynostosis and the Impact of Endoscopic Repair

spherical anisometropia.12 Amblyopia was found in 38% (15
of 39 patients), 80% of the time in the contralateral eye.12
Tarczy-Hornoch and colleagues found that 56% of 25 children with UCS had amblyogenic anisometropia, 79% on the
side opposite to the synostosis.13 A similar observation was
demonstrated by Macintosh and colleagues, who evaluated 52
patients with UCS and reported that 44% (25 patients) had
greater than 1 D of anisometropia.18 One retrospective study
of patients with UCS did not note an association of amblyopia
with anisometropia.19 Ethnic differences have been reported
by Tarczy-Hornoch and colleagues, who found a statistically
significant difference in the prevalence of amblyogenic anisometropia among Hispanic UCS patients (72%) compared with
non-Hispanic UCS patients (14%) (P = .02).13

The higher incidence of astigmatism in the eye contralateral to the fused suture in UCS has been explained by a
downward displacement of the orbital roof which may place
subtle pressure on the globe, altering corneal curvature.12 It
is well-known that vascular malformations or masses in the
eyelid produce secondary astigmatism by this mechanism
(pressure on the globe).20,21 Using 3-dimensional (3-D)
computed tomography (CT) images to study a cohort of
infants with UCS, Lo and colleagues demonstrated that
the two eyes have different height-to-width ratios (orbital
index).22
Metopic Craniosynostosis. In a retrospective series of
91 individuals who had isolated metopic craniosynostosis,
20.9% (19 patients) had astigmatism, 5.5% (5 patients)
had myopia, 5.5% (5 patients) had hyperopia, and 5.5%
(5 patients) had anisometropia. A total of eight patients
had amblyopia. Of these patients, amblyopia was caused
by a combination of refractive error and strabismus in
five patients and refractive error alone in three patients.23
Hypotelorism and upslanting of the lateral canthus is often
associated with metopic synostosis.24
Syndromic Craniosynostosis

In a retrospective series of 144 patients with syndromic
craniosynostosis including Apert, Crouzon, Pfeiffer,
and Saethre-Chotzen syndromes, Khan and colleagues
reported that 39.8% of patients had BCVA of 20/40 or
worse in the better seeing eye and 64.6% of patients had
BCVA of 20/40 or worse in at least one of their eyes.9
Forty percent of patients had 1 D or greater of astigmatism in one of their eyes. Oblique astigmatism was
present in at least one eye of 64% of these patients.9 The
prevalence of astigmatism differed from one syndrome
to another, with approximately 30% in Saethre-Chotzen
syndrome patients, 43% in Crouzon syndrome patients,
45% in Pfeiffer syndrome patients, and 52% in Apert
syndrome patients.9 The astigmatism may result, in
part, from the downward slant of the superior orbital
rim. Anisometropia of at least 1 D was found in 18%
of patients compared to 3.5% of age-matched children
without craniosynostosis. A review of 71 patients with
Crouzon syndrome by Gray and colleagues demonstrated
that 35% had vision loss in at least one eye and 9% had
bilateral visual impairment. Amblyopia was a common

cause of vision loss present in 21% of patients. Seventyseven patients had significant ametropia. Fifty-seven percent had hyperopia of at least 2 D and 20% had myopia
of at least 0.5 D.25 Consistent findings were reported by
Tay and colleagues in a retrospective series of 55 patients
with syndromic craniosynostosis (Apert, Crouzon, Pfeiffer, Saethre-Chotzen) or cranio-fronto-nasal dysplasia.16
Of these patients, 40% had visual impairment (35.5%
bilateral and 9.1% unilateral), and amblyopia (16.7%) as
well as ametropia (25%) were major risk factors.16 Forty
percent of patients with syndromic craniosynostosis had
astigmatism.16 There was a lower prevalence of hyperopia
(18%) compared to that reported by Gray and colleagues
(57%).16,25
Apert syndrome is mostly caused by fibroblast growth
factor receptor gene 2 (FGFR 2) point mutations, either
Ser252Trp or Pro253Arg. Patients with S252W mutation are far more likely to demonstrate significant astigmatism (82%) compared to those with P253R mutation
(14%) (P = .005).26 BCVA less than 20/40 in at least one
eye was more prevalent in the S252W mutation group
(60% compared with 12.5% in P253R mutation group)
(P < .05).27
Several theories have been proposed to explain the high
prevalence of astigmatism in syndromic craniosynostosis
including exposure keratopathy with subsequent corneal
distortion, ptosis especially in Saethre-Chotzen syndrome,
and orbital dysmorphology and asymmetry.9

Strabismus
Strabismus is a common oculomotor disorder in craniosynostosis, whether syndromic or nonsyndromic, with
reported incidence ranging from 39% to 91% compared
to an incidence of 2% to 3% in children under 6 years of
age without craniosynostosis.28–36 In nonsyndromic single
suture synostosis, it is most often found in patients with
UCS. Several genetic mutations have been associated with
craniosynostosis syndromes37,38 and some of these mutations appear to increase the risk of strabismus in these
sub-popuations.26 Strabismus is more common in Apert
syndrome patients with S252W mutation (91%) compared with those with the P253R mutation (85%) and
have a higher risk of superior rectus (SR) underaction (P
= .024) and are more frequently treated with strabismus
surgery (64% compared with 14% in P253R mutation
group) (P = .039).26 The strabismus can be horizontal, vertical, or both, but the most common pattern is V-pattern
strabismus characterized by horizontal, vertical, and torsional misalignment (Fig. 7.1). A similar study reported
that strabismus was more common in the S252W mutation group (47% compared with 39% in P253R mutation group); however, the difference was not statistically
significant (P = .546).27
Possible Mechanisms of Strabismus in Craniosynostosis

One or more of the following factors may explain the high
incidence of strabismus in patients with craniosynostosis.

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 7

Ophthalmic Complications of Craniosynostosis and the Impact of Endoscopic Repair

73

• Fig. 7.1 Patient with Apert Syndrome, Exorbitism, and Severe “See-Saw” V-Pattern Strabismus. He
has the marked excyclorotation of extraocular rectus muscles associated with this type of strabismus. In
addition, his midface retrusion results in increased contact of each globe on the adjacent inferior oblique
muscle, possibly exacerbating the V-pattern.

Absence or Dysgenesis of Extraocular Muscles

Several studies have documented absent or anomalous
extraocular muscles in Apert, Crouzon, and Pfeiffer syndromes. In Apert syndrome, Bustos and Donahue reported
a case of a 6-month-old patient with agenesis of inferior
and superior oblique muscles in both eyes.39 Several authors
have reported absent SR muscles.40–42 Helveston proposed
that a combination of amblyopia and significant horizontal strabismus in a patient with congenital superior oblique
(SO) palsy should raise the suspicion of an absent SO tendon.43 Pollard described 11 Apert syndrome patients with
bilateral SO palsy and significant horizontal strabismus in
primary and down gazes. Of these patients, five had no SO
tendon in either eye and two had only a small fibrous muscle remnant at the anticipated location.44 The same finding
has been described by Pinchoff and Sandall, who reported
two patients (one patient had Apert syndrome, the other
had Crouzon syndrome) both with absent SO tendons.43
Diamond and colleagues reported that 5 of 12 Crouzon
patients undergoing strabismus surgery had abnormal
extraocular muscles.45 Coats and colleagues evaluated 14
patients with craniosynostosis and noted bilateral anomalous SO tendon in 8 of these patients.46 Snir and colleagues
reported a 29-year-old Crouzon syndrome patient who
had bilateral agenesis of SR, inferior rectus (IR), SO, and
inferior oblique (IO) muscles together with abnormal wide
insertion of two horizontal muscles.47 Anomalous medial
rectus (MR) and lateral rectus (LR) muscles insertions have
been described by Caputo and Lingua in a Crouzon syndrome patient, whereas the MR was bifid, and twice the
normal size. Both MR and LR muscles were inserted 2.5
mm posterior to the limbus.48 Coats and Ou described a
Crouzon syndrome patient with bifid left MR insertion
with two muscle bellies separated by 5 mm of sclera. Both
bellies were inserted 9 mm from the limbus with a total
width of 13.5 mm.49 Margolis and colleagues examined IO
muscle from an Apert syndrome patient under light and
electron microscope. They demonstrated morphological
changes and structural alterations which may be responsible
for ocular motility disturbances even with grossly normal
looking muscle. Light microscopy disclosed enlarged hyalinized fibers with loss of myofibrillar organization. Under
electron microscopy, fragmented swollen mitochondria
with various nuclear abnormalities were seen.50 As FGFR2
has been demonstrated in human extraocular muscles, it is
likely that FGFR2 mutation may be directly responsible for

some of the extraocular muscle morphology reported.51 As
most studies reporting absence of extraocular muscles predate modern high resolution orbital imaging, it is plausible
that “absent” muscles may have been present, but thin and
dysmorphic and potentially identifiable with higher resolution magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) sequences.
Excyclorotation of Recti Muscles

The most common form of strabismus in patients with
craniosynostosis is V-pattern strabismus. Many factors
may contribute to the pathogenesis of V-pattern strabismus. Excyclorotation of the rectus muscles is responsible in
some cases. Excyclorotation is counter-clockwise rotation
of the rectus muscles of the right eye and a clockwise rotation of the rectus muscles of the left eye (Fig. 7.2). Using
orbital CT imaging, Dagi and colleagues evaluated extraocular muscle excyclorotation near the orbital apex (posterior orbit) and near the muscle pulleys (anterior orbit)
in patients with syndromic craniosynostosis (Apert, Crouzon, and Pfeiffer syndromes) and V-pattern strabismus.52
Patients classified with moderate-to-severe and severe (“seesaw”) V-pattern strabismus, demonstrated a significant correlation between the severity of V-pattern and the degree
of rectus muscle excyclorotation at both the anterior and
posterior orbital locations(Fig. 7.3).52 The degree of excyclorotation was significantly worse in patients with “seesaw” V-pattern strabismus (see Fig. 7.1) when compared
with those with moderate to severe strabismus. This correlation was not identified in patients with mild-to-moderate
V-pattern strabismus and is unlikely to be the cause in cases
with milder V-pattern.52 Severe or see-saw V-pattern strabismus is more common in Apert syndrome patients than
in those with Crouzon and Pfeiffer syndromes.52 This may
result from the distinctive orbital anatomy characterizing
each form of syndromic craniosynostosis. Apert syndrome
typically results in lateral orbital wall protrusion and ethmoidal sinus ballooning not seen in patients with Crouzon
or Pfeiffer syndromes.2,53,54 Weiss and colleagues demonstrated that excyclotorsion of rectus muscle pulleys changes
their impact on alignment in a gaze-dependent manner,
resulting in V-pattern exotropia in patients with Crouzon
syndrome.55
Retropositioning of Trochlea

In patients with UCS, the ipsilateral orbit has a characteristic “harlequin deformity” (Figs. 7.4–7.6) which results

in elevation and posterior displacement of the trochlea of
the SO. This desagittalization of the trochlea increases SO
laxity, thereby decreasing the muscle’s ability to incyclotort
and infraduct the eye. Retropositioning of the trochlea
can also occur during FOA. During FOA, the trochlea is
separated from its anatomical insertion and then passively
replaced, sometimes in a desagittalized position. Regardless of the cause of SO underaction, there is secondary
overaction of its opponent muscle, the inferior oblique
(IO).56 Asymmetry between SO and IO action often
results in V-pattern strabismus and, sometimes, secondary
ocular torticollis.57,58

• Fig. 7.5 External photo of patient with right unilateral coronal synostosis showing severe harlequin deformity on the right. The contralateral
(left) side of the skull has compensatory growth and inferior displacement of the orbital roof causing significant astigmatism of the left eye.

• Fig. 7.6 External photo from above showing frontal expansion of the
skull on the side opposite the synostosis.

Other Theories

It has been proposed that anatomic orbital changes may
result in enhanced contact between IO and the undersurface
of the globe. Such changes may result in overaction of the

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 7

75

• Fig. 7.7 Patient with Treacher Collins syndrome and acute worsening of strabismus when hardware migration resulted in penetration and fixation
of the right globe.

• Fig. 7.8 Torticollis Associated with Craniosynostosis. Such patients

may have ocular torticollis, a head tilt to enable binocular fusion, along
with a non-ocular component.

IO and underaction of the SO with resultant V-pattern.58,59
Denis and colleagues proposed that fronto-zygomatic displacement of the lateral orbit in these cases increases the
tension on both IR and IO muscles resulting in overaction
of these muscles and V-pattern strabismus.60 Late migration of surgical screws into orbit may result in vision loss or
induced strabismus61 (Fig. 7.7).

Patterns and Management of Strabismus in
Craniosynostosis
V-pattern strabismus, the most common form of strabismus in craniosynostosis, is a horizontal deviation with an
increase in the angle of strabismus in up gaze compared to
down gaze of at least 15 prism diopters (PD).62 Normal
ocular alignment is essential for the development of binocular single vision and stereopsis. A common associated
finding in this population is ocular torticollis (head tilt to
enable binocular fusion) often exacerbated by non-ocular
torticollis (Fig. 7.8). Normalization of ductions and versions by strabismus surgery will reduce torticollis if it is

ocular in nature, but the results may prove disappointing
if the child has lost binocularity due to amblyopia and suppression. Strabismus surgery to improve ocular alignment
in such cases may minimally reduce torticollis. Strabismus
surgery in the population of patients with craniosynostosis, especially syndromic, is much more challenging than in
the general population. Techniques described include IO
weakening procedures, SO tucking, adjustment for seemingly absent extraocular muscles by transposition of others, and relocation of excyclorotated muscles and aberrant
insertions (Fig. 7.9).5,45,63,64 The best timing for strabismus
surgery depends on a several factors including age, presence
or absence of binocular potential, and other impending surgery. An approach to reducing the V-pattern includes consideration of the gaze of maximum misalignment (Knapp
classification),65 presence of see-saw pattern51 (see Fig. 7.1),
the presence or absence of binocular fusion or fusional
potential, anomalous anatomy or position of extraocular
muscles, angulation of the orbital bones possibly resulting
in extraocular muscles excyclorotation,51 and orbital asymmetry.5 Preoperative orbital imaging to evaluate the relative
degree of excyclorotation, and position of the recti and the
trajectory of the SO, can provide important information
for surgical planning.5 High-resolution MRI is most useful
for visualizing extraocular muscle detail and CT for orbital
changes. 3-D ultrasound can provide additional information.66 Although imaging provides accurate information on
extraocular muscle location and trajectory, nothing substitutes for insights gained by intraoperative exaggerated traction testing of the SO and IO, forced ductions of the rectus
muscles, and intraoperative view of the quality and trajectory of the extraocular muscles.

Papilledema and Optic Neuropathy
Craniosynostosis affects skull growth which may result in
intracranial hypertension. Increased intracranial pressure
(ICP) may result from abnormalities within the intracranial
venous system as well. A hallmark of elevated ICP is the
development of papilledema (Figs. 7.10 and 7.11) which
sometimes is accompanied by new intraocular myelin
migration (Fig. 7.12). Chronic papilledema and elevation
in ICP result in optic atrophy and permanent vision loss
ensues (Fig. 7.13).
Nonsyndromic Craniosynostosis

Prevalence of increased ICP and papilledema varies according to the affected suture. Intracranial hypertension is more
prevalent in patients with complex and multiple suture

in multisuture syndromic craniosynostosis to 31.3% in
brachycephaly.67
Sagittal Synostosis (Scaphocephaly). Sagittal synostosis
left untreated will result in intracranial hypertension and
papilledema in 10% to 20% of cases.68–70 The prevalence
of papilledema in sagittal synostosis varies according to the

type and timing of craniofacial (CF) repair. It ranges from
2.5% to 10% prior to CF repair and 2% to 9% after CF
repair. Van Veelen and colleagues demonstrated a 9% incidence of papilledema in patients with sagittal synostosis
patients having extended strip craniectomy. It was more
frequent in patients who also developed premature closure of a coronal suture.69 Papilledema was more likely in
patients with headache, frontal bulge, and with decrease of
head circumference by 1 standard deviation or greater.69
In a retrospective study comparing fronto-biparietal

In one patient, papilledema resolved spontaneously after 2
months, whereas the other patient had long-standing papilledema and subsequently underwent a second surgery.73
Prevalence of papilledema in patients with metopic synostosis (trigonocephaly), who tend to have similar growth pattern as those with UCS, has been reported to be as low as
1.5%.74 Out of 176 patients with trigonocephaly, 16 had
occipitofrontal head circumference curve stagnation. Only

remodeling with and without introduction of a bony
bridge to increase the skull width in patients with sagittal
synostosis, papilledema occurred with the same frequency
in both cohorts (P = .1).71 Lower incidence of papilledema
has been reported with spring-assisted minimally invasive
strip craniotomy (2.4%) compared with early extended
strip correction (9%).72
Abnormal venous anatomy may be, in part, responsible for
the higher incidence of elevated ICP associated with sagittal synostosis compared with other forms of single suture synostosis.68
Unicoronal Synostosis (UCS) and Metopic Single Suture
Synostosis. Papilledema is a relatively rare finding in patients
with isolated UCS. In a retrospective study conducted by
Beeten and colleagues, none of 67 patients had papilledema
when they underwent CF repair before the age of 1 and

Several theories have been hypothesized to explain the high
incidence of elevated ICP in syndromic craniosynostosis.
Beyond the impact of premature fusion of multiple sutures
resulting in a disproportionally small intracranial space,
abnormal cerebral venous anatomy and drainage appears
to play a significant role.75,76 Hayward hypothesized several mechanisms that might be responsible for anomalous
venous drainage. His constriction theory states that abnormal bony anatomy at the skull base restricts venous outflow.
Venous drainage via transosseous and mastoid emissary
collaterals may result in elevated venous pressure. This collateral circulation evolves as a result of failed development
of the sigmoid sinus and jugular bulb reflecting insufficient
postpartum maturation and persistence of a fetal pattern
of intracranial venous drainage.76 In addition, anomalous
venous drainage might occur as a result of FGFR gene mutations.76 Taylor and colleagues demonstrated that anomalous venous drainage is a major contributor to elevated
ICP in these patients.75 In a prospective study including
62 patients with syndromic craniosynostosis using papilledema at fundoscopy to evaluate the presence of intracranial
hypertension, Spruijt and colleagues reported that 33.9%
had intracranial hypertension which significantly correlated
with the presence of moderate to severe obstructive sleep
apnea (39% of the cohort) (P = .007) and occipitofrontal
head circumference growth arrest (P = .006).77 It should
be noted that although papilledema is considered the most
reliable screening method for intracranial hypertension
detection, its sensitivity varies based on the patients’ age.
Tuite and colleagues reported a 100% sensitivity in children
over 8 years but only 22% in younger children.78 As chronic
papilledema results in optic atrophy (see Fig. 7.13), studies
reporting only papilledema and not optic atrophy may significantly underestimate the incidence of optic neuropathy.

Obstructive Sleep Apnea and Optic Neuropathy
Obstructive sleep apnea is a common finding in patients
with syndromic craniosynostosis. The prevalence and severity vary according to the phenotype and the degree of midfacial hypoplasia, with highest prevalence in Apert syndrome
patients (80%) followed by patients with Pfeiffer syndrome
(72%). In a prospective electrophysiological study of patients
with severe obstructive sleep apnea, Liguori and colleagues
demonstrated a significant latency delay together with
decreased P100 wave amplitude on visual evoked potential

booksmedicos.org

78 C HA P T ER 7

Ophthalmic Complications of Craniosynostosis and the Impact of Endoscopic Repair

• Fig. 7.14 Patient with Pfeiffer syndrome with exorbitism with midfacial retrusion and risk of ruptured globe
in addition to exposure keratopathy.

(VEP) at all spatial frequencies indicating that obstructive
sleep apnea can cause optic nerve dysfunction.79 It has been
proposed that the combination of intracranial hypertension
and obstructive sleep apnea increase the risk of optic nerve
impairment and vision loss in patients with craniosynostosis. Dagi and colleagues used pattern reversal VEP (prVEP)
to evaluate the visual function in patients with craniosynostosis, comparing those with and without obstructive sleep
apnea. They found a significant association of the combined
history of obstructive sleep apnea and elevated intracranial
hypertension with prolonged P100 latency on prVEP and
lower BCVA.80

Detecting Optic Neuropathy in Craniosynostosis:
Spectral Domain Optical Coherence Tomography
(SD-OCT), Visual Field Testing, and prVEP
In a retrospective study evaluating 54 craniosynostosis
patients, 13 of 54 had some degree of optic neuropathy on
fundoscopy (8 had optic atrophy and 5 had papilledema).
SD-OCT showed thinner than normative range retinal
nerve fiber layer (RNFL) in patients with optic atrophy
and thicker than normative range RNFL in patients with
papilledema, as would be predicted. SD-OCT sensitivity
for optic atrophy detection was 88% (specificity was 94%);
for papilledema it was 60% (specificity was 90%); and for
either form of optic neuropathy it was 77% (specificity was
83%).81
The correlation of visual field abnormalities with fundus
features compatible with optic neuropathy was lower, with
visual field features demonstrating sensitivity of 60% and
a specificity of 81%. The study concluded that SD-OCT
RNFL thickness is more sensitive to detect optic neuropathy in craniosynostosis patients than visual field testing
(P = .002).81
Several studies have demonstrated that prVEP may
detect early visual deterioration in patients with craniosynostosis. Liasis and colleagues evaluated the visual function
in eight patients with syndromic craniosynostosis by serial
visual acuity measurements, optic nerve appearance, and
prVEP before and after CF repair. They showed that N80
and P100 amplitudes tend to decrease before CF repair and
increased in 6 of 8 patients after CF repair, suggesting that
visual dysfunction may improve in these children after CF
repair and demonstrating the utility of prVEP surveillance
monitoring.82

Exposure Keratopathy

Higher rates of proptosis and subsequent exposure keratopathy have been reported with Pfeiffer syndrome, especially
when compared with other craniosynostosis syndromes
(Fig. 7.14). In a retrospective study conducted by Sharma
and colleagues including 22 patients with Pfeiffer syndrome, 21 patients had proptosis, which was severe (>25
mm exophthalmos) in 59% of cases, with four eyes having
had at least one attack of globe luxation. Out of 21 patients,
9 had bilateral exposure keratopathy at the time of presentation.83 Visually significant corneal opacity as a result
of corneal exposure was responsible for vision loss at the
last follow-up in six patients. All these patients had severe
corneal exposure (corneal ulcer within visual axis and/or
corneal ulcer >3 mm in diameter, infectious keratopathy,
or corneal perforation) at initial presentation. Extremely
severe proptosis has been reported with type 2 Pfeiffer syndrome.84 Tarsorrhaphy may be essential in cases of exposure
keratopathy to maintain the integrity of the ocular surface
and allow for adequate corneal protection (Fig. 7.15). Several tarsorrhaphy techniques, whether temporary or permanent, have been described. The choice depends mainly
on whether the corneal exposure is acute with anticipated
resolution or is chronic in nature. Temporary tarsorrhaphy
can be simply achieved by applying adhesive tape from the
upper eyelid to the cheek. Botulinum toxin can be injected
under the preseptal skin of the upper lid achieving ptosis
for up to 6 weeks; however, this method is not preferred
because of the risk of amblyopia. Suture tarsorrhaphy can
be performed using absorbable suture placed in the lateral
one-third of the palpebral fissure in anticipation of postoperative lagophthalmos after craniofacial surgery; however, if
longer eyelid closure is needed, nonabsorbable sutures such
as polypropylene should be used. In the setting of severe
lagophthalmos and anticipated chronic corneal exposure,
permanent tarsorrhaphy may be considered by surgically
debriding the mucocutaneous junction of both the upper
and lower lids, creating permanent adhesions.7 Purushothaman and Sujatha reported a 3-year-old Crouzon syndrome
boy who presented with bilateral subluxated globes for
which emergency lateral tarsorrhaphies were done.85 Repositioning of the lid and conjunctiva can reverse conjunctival
exposure (Fig. 7.16). Lacrimal gland prolapse is an uncommon feature of craniosynostosis. It usually occurs as a result
of decreased orbital volume leading to increased posterior

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 7

Ophthalmic Complications of Craniosynostosis and the Impact of Endoscopic Repair

79

• Fig. 7.15 Left eye of child with Pfeiffer Syndrome before (left side) and after (right side) lateral tarsorrhaphy

to protect against globe injury. Note that after tarsorrhaphy, the reduction in photophobia and exposure
keratopathy facilitated an optimal refraction and glasses were provided to correct the significant astigmatic
error, along with hearing aids (not shown).

• Fig. 7.16 Patient with left eye lower lid ectropion and resultant conjunctival exposure before (left) and after (right) repair

orbital pressure.86 Jordan and colleagues reported their
surgical experience in the management of lacrimal gland
prolapse associated with craniosynostosis syndromes. They
found that with lateral orbital wall advancement, spontaneous repositioning of lacrimal gland may occur, obviating the
need for an additional procedure.86

Anterior Segment Anomalies
In most of patients with syndromic craniosynostosis, the
predominant anterior segment concern is exposure keratopathy due to midface retrusion and secondary exorbitism.
There are rare cases, however, where distinctive anterior segment congenital anomalies have been reported.
Shotelersuk and colleagues reported a 15-year-old patient
who had an FGFR2 mutation and who presented with corneal limbal stem cells deficiency (LSCD).87 The same mutation has been proposed as the cause of an abnormal eye
development in a Pfeiffer syndrome patient who presented
with LSCD, microcornea, and congenital glaucoma.88
Two additional Pfeiffer syndrome patients were reported
to have atypical iris colobomata superiorly, corectopia and
superior LSCD.89 Schultz and colleagues have reported a
patient with syndromic multisuture craniosynostosis who
had severe corneal decompensation, anterior segment dysgenesis and optic nerve hypoplasia.90 Ectopia lentis (subluxation of crystalline lenses usually due to loose or defective
zonular fibers) has been described in rare cases. Cruysberg
and colleagues reported monozygotic twins with craniosynostosis who had CF surgery and then, at the age of 3 years,
both presented with bilateral ectopia lentis.91 Reichel and
colleagues reported a 23-year-old patient with oxycephaly
who had subluxated lenses in both eyes and total retinal

detachment in one eye.92 Quercia and Teebi described autosomal dominant syndrome of UCS, ectopia lentis, myopia
and congenital heart defects. This association should be
kept in mind for genetic counseling of these patients.93

Ptosis and Lid Anomalies
Saethre Chotzen syndrome is commonly associated with
symmetric or asymmetric ptosis, often warranting repair
(Fig. 7.17). Lid anomalies have more rarely been reported
in Michels syndrome, chromosome 7p partial deletion,
chromosome 11q partial deletion, and chromosome 13q
partial deletion.94,95 Lid anomalies in these conditions
include those seen in more common forms of multisuture
synostosis such as ptosis with downward slanting of the
eyelids and orbital hypertelorism (Fig. 7.18) and telecanthus (Fig. 7.19).

Hypotelorism
In metopic craniosynostosis, premature metopic suture
fusion results in arrest of forehead transverse growth resulting in a triangular shaped forehead (trigonocephaly) and a
subnormal distance between medial orbital walls (orbital
hypotelorism)24,96 (Fig. 7.20). It is usually associated with
deficient lateral superior orbital rims with upslanting of
lateral canthi and the lateral portion of both eyebrows.96,97
Hinojosa and colleagues demonstrated that 40% of cases
had epicanthal folds which may hide the presence of hypotelorism.98 Nguyen and colleagues compared the effect of
FOA versus an endoscopic approach with postoperative helmet therapy on hypotelorism and trigonocephaly in patients
with metopic synostosis. At 1-year postoperative follow-up,

Impact of Endoscopic Repair on Ophthalmic
Outcomes in Children with Unicoronal Synostosis
From an ophthalmic stand point, the optimal CF repair
in patients with UCS, whether by early ESC and helmet
therapy or later FOA, remains subject to debate in spite of
significant evidence supporting both excellent craniofacial
and superior ophthalmic outcomes associated with early
endoscopic repair.100–103 Several reports have demonstrated
reduced rates of anisoastigmatism and V-pattern strabismus incidence and severity in children with UCS treated

by earlier ESC and helmet therapy compared with those
treated by FOA. MacKinnon and colleagues evaluated rates
of anisoastigmatism in 37 UCS patients and reported no significant difference in the mean anisoastigmatism in children
treated by ESC (11 patients) compared with those treated
by FOA (26 patients) before 2 years of age, but by about 2
years of age, 27% of children treated in the FOA group had
severe strabismus whereas none treated by ESC had severe
strabismus (P = .056).104 Moderate to severe fundus torsion was more common in patients treated by FOA than by
ESC.104 A larger comparative retrospective case series was
conducted by the same group evaluating 43 patients. Of
these 43 patients, 21 were treated by ESC with a mean follow-up of 23.5 months compared with 22 treated by FOA
with a mean follow-up of 21.5 months.102 The standard
deviation of anisoastigmatism was higher in the FOA group
(0.92) compared to ESC group (0.51) (P = .04).102 None
in the ESC group developed severe V-pattern whereas 32%
of those treated by FOA developed severe V-pattern at final
follow-up visit (P < .0001). Fig. 7.21 demonstrates mildto-absent V-pattern strabismus typical for a child treated by
ESC, and Fig. 7.9 demonstrates notable V-pattern strabismus associated with treatment by FOA. There was higher
incidence of significant ocular torticollis in patients treated
by FOA (12 patients) compared with ESC and helmet therapy (only 2 patients) (P = .004). More patients treated by
FOA (9 patients) underwent strabismus surgery compared
with children treated by ESC (2 patients) (P = .03) and
there was a significantly higher rate of amblyopia in patients
treated by FOA (10 patients) compared with those treated
by ESC (only 2 patients) (P = .002).102 Similar results
were found by Isaac and colleagues who evaluated 94 UCS
patients (60 treated by ESC and 34 treated by FOA) with a
median age of 46.5 months at the last follow-up visit in the

booksmedicos.org

Ophthalmic Complications of Craniosynostosis and the Impact of Endoscopic Repair

B

81

C

• Fig. 7.21 Unicoronal Synostosis Treated by ESC and Orthosis. Typically, no significant V-pattern strabismus ensues. ESC, Endoscopic strip craniectomy.

ESC group compared with 64 months in the FOA group
(P = .06).100 Severe V-pattern strabismus was significantly
more prevalent in patients treated by FOA. Sixty-five percent of those treated by FOA required strabismus surgery
compared with 31% of those treated by ESC. Rates of strabismus reoperation were higher in patients treated by FOA
(P = .001).100 In addition, the much shorter length of the
procedure and the reduced blood loss associated with treatment by ESC was clearly demonstrated.100 In the largestto-date retrospective age-matched comparative study of
long-term ophthalmic outcomes associated with treating
UCS with FOA versus ESC and orthosis, Elhusseiny and
colleagues demonstrated improved short- and long-term
ophthalmic outcomes of UCS patients treated by ESC and
orthosis compared to patients treated by FOA.105 They
evaluated 120 UCS patients (60 treated by ESC and helmet
therapy and 60 treated by FOA) at an early postprocedure
examination at approximately 18 months of age and at a
later postprocedure examination close to 60 months of age.
By 60 months of age, the incidence of significant anisoastigmatism (72% in the FOA group compared with 46% in the
ESC group, P < .0001) and the median anisoastigmatism
(−0.75±1.1 in the FOA group compared with −0±0.5 in the
ESC group, P < .001) were significantly higher in patients
treated by FOA. The prevalence of severe V-pattern strabismus was significantly higher in patients treated by FOA
compared with ESC at both early (10% in FOA versus 0%
in ESC, P = .029) and late (36% in FOA versus 12% in
ESC, P = .015) examinations. The odds ratio for having
strabismus surgery was 2.8 for those treated by FOA compared with ESC. Twenty-two percent of patients treated by
FOA required multiple strabismus surgeries compared with
8% of those treated by ESC (P < .001).105 The most common surgeries performed were IO weakening and vertical
muscle surgeries. SO tucking procedures, typically reserved
for those with the most severe V-patterns, were only performed in patients who had been treated by FOA (seven
patients).
Improved ophthalmic outcomes in patients with UCS
managed by ESC compared with FOA may result from the
earlier timing of ESC or because during FOA the trochlea may be disturbed, or both. ESC is usually performed
before the age of 4 months, typically before the onset of
ocular misalignment. In contrast, FOA is usually performed
at about 10 months of age when strabismus has already
developed. Once strabismus develops, it often worsens over
time. If an intervention can prevent the onset, the prognosis improves.102,106 In addition, in the course of performing

FOA, the trochlea of the SO is separated from its insertion along the medial orbital wall. Although it is passively
replaced later in the procedure, it is not specifically secured
and retro-positioning or back-slipping may occur, resulting
in SO laxity, under-action, and V-pattern strabismus.5 New
onset of strabismus post-FOA has been well demonstrated.32
The impact of early endoscopic repair compared to
later cranial vault repair on the ophthalmic outcomes of
patients with multiple suture synostosis is currently under
investigation.
A literature review of PubMed, CENTRAL, Google Scholar,
ClinicalTrials.gov (www.clinicaltrials.gov), Ovid MEDLINE
In-Process, Ovid MEDLINE, EMBASE, and other NonIndexed Citations was performed for all articles discussing the
ophthalmic complications associated with craniosynostosis
whether syndromic or nonsyndromic and the impact of different procedures of craniofacial repair on the ophthalmic outcomes. There were no language or date restrictions.

8

Sagittal Craniosynostosis
DAVID F. JIMENEZ, MD, FACS

Introduction
Sagittal suture craniosynostosis is the most common type
of sutural stenosis and affects males more than females at a
rate of 3.5:1.1,2 It is a genetic condition with which a number of genes have been associated, and they include: ALX₄3;
BBS94; BPM24; ERF5; FGR16; FGFR27; nonetheless,
most patients presenting with isolated single suture synostosis are completely normal otherwise and do not present in
association with syndromes. IGF1R8; MSX29; PTHZR10;
S1X211; SMAD612; and TWIST1.13 The classic clinical
presentation is commonly described as scaphocephaly or
dolichocephaly. Due to bone growth restriction in a vector
perpendicular to the suture, continued normal compensatory brain growth leads to bifrontal bossing and forehead
prominence. Additionally, there is marked occipital protrusion and skull elongation in the anterior-posterior axis. The
parietal eminences are constricted and there is slanting and
downward angulation of the parietal bones. Depending on
what part of the suture is closed, the overall head shape will
vary and a number of different phenotypic presentations
may occur (Fig. 8.1). As previously stated, numerous procedures have been developed to treat this condition. Simple
and complex strip craniectomies have been done since the
1890s. A number of calvarial vault remodeling surgeries are
currently being performed worldwide by a large number
of craniofacial centers. Due to the nature of the condition,
a simple linear cut of the stenosed suture will lead to very
rapid restenosis. A small strip craniectomy will have the
same fate a few weeks later. Because we operate on young
infants, our approach has been to make wider craniectomies. Thus, the craniectomy size and width is inversely proportional to the patient’s age.

Patient Positioning
The patient’s position of choice is the modified prone or
“sphynx” position. Adequate and secure positioning can be
achieved with a number of different frameworks, including headrest such as the pediatric Doro cranial stabilization system (Pro Med Instruments GmbH, Freiburg,
Germany). Our method of choice has been the use of

a suction stabilized beanbag (Fig. 8.2), which has a great
level of flexibility for contouring. A thin viscoelastic pad
is placed on top of the beanbag, followed by a clean bed
sheet. A Bair Hugger pediatric heating blanket is placed on
the patient for temperature control (3M, St. Paul, MN).
After endotracheal intubation, a coordinated patient move
is done in conjunction with the anesthesia team to place
the patient prone on the beanbag at the end of the surgical
table (placed at 180 degrees from anesthesia end; Fig. 8.3).
The patient’s neck is extended so that the top of the head
is parallel to the floor. Cast padding is carefully placed on
all potential pressure points, such as the shoulders, elbows,
knees, and ankles. Once the patient is placed in the sphynx
position, the anesthesia team must listen for breath sounds
and make absolutely sure that they are equally bilateral. The
endotracheal (ET) tube needs to be introduced deeper than
normal so that it moves to the appropriate location after
neck hyperextension. If proper ET tube positioning is not
procured, ventilator problems will occur after the incision is
made, which can lead to emergent break down of the sterile surgical field and even the need for reintubation of the
patient. Every effort is made to secure the ET tube and ventilator circuit to the beanbag and table in order to prevent
inadvertent extubation. A throat pack is routinely inserted
to improve tube stability and minimize air leaks.
Because of the patient’s location in relation to the heart
(higher), the risk of venous air embolism (VAE) occurring is
relatively high. It may occur directly into the sagittal sinus,
a detached bridging vein, a contrical vein, or a large osseous channel. If large enough, a VAE can have a significant
negative impact and may even be fatal. As such, it is imperative that monitoring for VAE during the entire procedure be
instituted. The simplest and best way to monitor for such an
event is to use a precordial doppler (Fig. 8.4A). The sensor is
placed on the left side of the chest in front of the heart and
maximal signal pick-up is aided with a lubricant (Fig. 8.4B).
Once a stable signal is obtained, a 4- × 4-inch gauze is placed
on top of the sensor and the entire unit is secured in place
with foam tape (Fig. 8.4C). To document that the system
is properly functioning, the anesthesia team rapidly flushes
a milliliter of saline through an IV line and the subsequent
echo signal is heard, which indicates a functioning system.

• Fig. 8.1 (A–F) Patients with sagittal craniosynostosis present with different phenotypic head shapes and

deformities depending on which part of the suture is closed. The most common type has a very prominent
forehead and point occipital. Other forms demonstrate a long rectangular shape. Other patients present
with an ovoid shape.

The surgical setup and draping are shown on Fig. 8.5.
After draping with towels, a 1016 self-adhesive collecting
pouch is placed around the patient’s head and a split drape
is positioned under the pouch. Cottonoids and different
sizes of Gelfoam pieces are placed behind the patient’s head
for quick and easy surgeon access. Self-adhering instrument pouches are placed on either side of the head. The
right pouch holds the suction cautery, Bovie hand piece,
bipolar, and suction. The left pouch holds another suction
(large diameter) and a second Bovie hand piece. To minimize cable entanglement, a towel is stapled to the drapes
over all of the running lines and cables.

Incisions
In order to completely release the stenosed sagittal suture,
the exposed surgical field should extend from the anterior

fontanelle to the lambda. Even though the exposure can be
done with a single incision midway between these points,
we have found that it is much safer to do so via two separate incisions. At the beginning of our series, we placed
the anterior incision directly over the anterior fontanelle,
but later modified it to be approximately 2 to 3 cm behind
the fontanelle (Fig. 8.6). This configuration has several
advantages. It decreases the distance between the incisions
and makes endoscopic manipulation easier. It places the
incision away from the coronal suture, thereby minimizing
the risk of dural tears when doing the wedge osteotomies.
In case of the occasional durotomy, the two incisions give
greater access to the dura for primary repair and closure.
The incisions are made with the Bovie needle tip coagulation set on the cut mode at 15 watts. When done properly,
the dermis is cleanly incised and there is no burning or
damage to this skin layer (Fig. 8.7). Deeper dissection is

done with the needle tip on the coagulation mode set at
20 watts. Because of the scalp’s mobility, the incisions need
not be long and usually suffice at about 3 cm ± 1 cm. At
the end of the procedure, the scalp incisions are infiltrated
with bupivacaine 0.25% with epinephrine 1:250,000 at
1 cc/kg. The incision is closed with 4-0 monocryl interrupted galeal sutures and the dermis sealed with Mastisol
and Steri-strips. The patient’s head is not shaved or hair
clipped except for a very small strip at the site of the incision. Large amounts of hair can be covered and stapled
out of the way (Fig. 8.8). The posterior incision is placed
immediately in front of the lambda with its epicenter over
the sagittal suture (Fig. 8.9).

using the monopolar needle tip coagulator (Fig. 8.10). The

• Fig. 8.4 (A) After intubation and prior to placing the patient in the modified prone (sphynx) position, a

rhinoplasty lighted retractor is used to elevate the scalp and
expose the loose areolar tissue plane. If two surgeons are
operating, they can simultaneously open both incisions and
develop the subgaleal plane as needed with the help of an
assistant (Fig. 8.11), which will, of course, decrease surgical
operative time. Typically, a number of small perforating vessels are seen and sequentially coagulated (Fig. 8.12). These
vessels are more prominent and engorged when there is an
increase in intracranial pressure. If properly done, complete
dissection of this plane is developed in a bloodless fashion,
extending from anterior fontanelle to lambda and 3 cm on
either side of the midline.

Osteotomies
Development of the anterior transverse osteotomy is begun
with a paramedian burr hole done with a pediatric craniotome (7 mm) perforator. The burr hole is placed to the left
of the stenosed suture 2 or 3 cm from the midline, depending on the patient’s age. Bony hemostasis is obtained with
bone wax and/or Surgiflo. A #1 Penfield dissector (cupped
end) is inserted under the bone and gently passed towards

the midline, developing an epidural plane. A 4- or 5-mm
Kerrison rongeur is sequentially used to create the anterior osteotomy across the midline and sagittal sinus (Fig.
8.13). Bony hemostasis is attained with a combination of
suction electrocautery (60 watts) of the osteotomized bone,
Gelfoam, and pressure with cottonoids. The #1 Penfield is
placed under the bone and advanced anteriorly to reach
the anterior fontanelle. Direct palpation of the scalp corroborates the placement of the #1 Penfield to the dura at
the fontanelle (Fig. 8.14A). A triangular section of bone
is removed by carefully placing the Mayo scissors and cutting the bone from the anterior osteotomy to the fontanelle extending from the lateral aspect of the osteotomy on
each side (Fig. 8.14B). Removal of this piece of triangular
bone (Fig. 8.14C) allows for placement and insertion of the
endoscope under the bone posteriorly (Fig. 8.14D). A second transverse osteotomy is done immediately in front of
the lambda. In a similar fashion, a burr hole is placed on
the left side of the posterior incision and a Kerrison rongeur
is used to remove the bone in a piecemeal fashion towards
the midline. Extreme care is taken when crossing the stenosed suture in the midline. The stenosed sagittal suture

at this level commonly has a deep bony keel that indents
the sagittal sinus (Fig. 8.15). Tearing the sinus at this point
of the procedure could have dire consequences given the
limited exposure of the sinus. If a prominent bony keel is
encountered, it is safest to make a second burr hole on the
contralateral side and remove bone from that direction. This
maneuver leaves the keel exposed on both sides and makes it
easier and safer to pass a #1 Penfield under it to fully free the
sinus. Once fully dissected, the bone is completely removed,
thereby completing the osteotomy.

Epidural Dissection

• Fig. 8.6 Top view of an infant’s head with the anterior and posterior

incisions marked with solid lines. The dotted lines delineate the anterior fontanelle and coronal sutures anteriorly, and sagittal and lambdoid
sutures posteriorly. The center of the incisions is over the sagittal suture.
This configuration gives complete access to the entire sagittal suture.

Having removed the triangular piece of bone anteriorly, a
rigid 4-mm, 30-degree endoscope is used to visualize and
develop the epidural plane. The angulated end is directed
towards the overlying bone. The dura anterior to the osteotomy can be gently depressed with the endoscope as it
is advanced posteriorly towards the lambda (Fig. 8.16). A
right-handed surgeon handles the endoscope with the left
hand and uses the right hand to manipulate a 7-French
insulated semirigid suction. The suction is moved in tandem with the endoscope, being used as a dural dissector
and placed several centimeters in front of the scope (Fig.
8.17). As the suction is moved posteriorly and from side
to side, the endoscope closely follows. The endoscope is
mounted inside an irrigation sheath connected to a 60-cc
syringe filled with saline and controlled by an assistant. If
desired and available, the irrigating port and tube can be
attached to a motorized irrigating unit which the surgeon
can control with a foot pedal. The intermittent saline flushes
serve to clear any blood on the endoscope or surgical field,
which is then removed with the suction tube. Not uncommonly, small bridging veins extending from the sagittal
sinus to the overlying bone can be encountered. They may

Paramedian Osteotomies
The vertex craniectomy is roughly shaped like a rectangle
with the shorter ends being the anterior and posterior
osteotomies just created, while the paramedian osteotomies make up longer sides of the rectangle. As previously
stated, in the younger patients (<6 months), these cuts on
the bone can be quickly and easily made with curved or
straight Mayo scissors (Fig. 8.19A). Care is taken to make
sure that the dura has been adequately separated from
the bone prior to making the cuts. The first cut extends
to halfway or three-quarters of the way to the lambda.
A quick look with the endoscope can assure that there

Sagittal Craniosynostosis

91

• Fig. 8.13 Ragnell retractors hold skin edges to show the anterior
osteotomy (5 mm) with the cauterized bone edges. The epicenter of
the osteotomy is over the sagittal sinus.

are not dural injuries and that the rest of the epidural
space back to the posterior osteotomy is freed. The rest
of the osteotomy is finished with the scissors. An identical maneuver is made on the contralateral side, thereby
releasing the entire block of bone from the cranium. Most
commonly, the bone can be removed through the anterior
incision in-toto (Fig. 8.19B), or if need be, it may be
cut in half and removed in two pieces. A large piece of
Gelfoam soaked in Thrombin is then placed on top of the
dura and gentle pressure applied to the scalp overlying
it. After a minute or two, the Gelfoam is removed, the
surgical field is copiously irrigated with saline and once
again inspected for bleeding points. At this stage of the
operation, we proceed to make the barrel-stave osteotomies. It is our belief that by creating these osteotomies
bilaterally, the brain is able to expand with less pressure
on the parietal bones and correction takes place sooner
(Fig. 8.20). Theoretically, as all other sutures are open,
the expansion should take place without the stave osteotomies. However, in our hands all four osteotomies can
be done in about 5 minutes, without delaying the case,
thereby allowing us to do them safely.
The first maneuver consists of sliding a coated/insulated brain ribbon under the bone to separate the dura
safely from the bone (Fig. 8.21A). Next, a closed pair of
Mayo scissors are slid under the galea directly over the
location where the dura was just separated from the bone
(Fig. 8.21B). The endoscope can then be used to visualize the epidural plane from the incision down to squamosal suture (Fig. 8.21C). Once the dura has been cleared,
a wedge-shaped section of bone is resected to create the
barrel stave osteotomy (Fig. 8.21D). The osteotomy locations are as follows: (1) lateral to the anterior osteotomy, 1
or 2 cm behind the coronal suture on the right; (2) lateral
and anterior to the posterior osteotomy, staying away from

the lambdoid suture (Fig. 8.21E). Similar and symmetric
osteotomies mirror the location on the left side. After placing the brain ribbon and separating the dura, the curved
Mayo scissors (closed tips) (Fig. 8.21F) are advanced along

and away from the lambdoid suture and underlying transverse venous sinus. The scissors are used to cut the wedge
osteotomy as was done anteriorly and Surgiflo is used for
hemostasis. The exact same maneuver is done on the left
side, thereby releasing the parietal bones from the occipital
and frontal bones bilaterally. Following the original bone
cuts and wedge osteotomies, the edges of the remaining
bone produce sharp and pointed right angle corners that
can place undue pressure points on the overlying scalp
(Fig. 8.22). The best way to deal with these sharp edges
is to remove them using Mayo scissors (Fig. 8.23A–C).
At the end, there should be smooth and rounded edges as
shown in Fig. 8.23D.

The J&B dural retractor is loaded with the zero-degree
endoscope and the blades brought close together and
inserted through the anterior incision. The blades are then
opened and separated to retract the scalp superiorly and
the dura inferiorly. The cut edges of the bone are placed
between the blades and the suction electrocautery is used
to slowly cauterize the bleeding edges (Fig. 8.24A). The
entire J&B retractor and cautery unit are moved in unison
along the lateral edges of the vertex craniectomy until all
bony bleeding is stopped (Fig. 8.24B,C). This process is
continued in a circumferential fashion to include the wedge
osteotomies. The anterior and posterior bone edges can be
cauterized under direct visualization and without the need

and dried. Mastisol is placed alongside the incisions and,

the beanbag is deflated and the patient quickly placed in a
supine position. Before extubation, blood is obtained for an
immediate postoperative hemoglobin and hematocrit. After
extubation the patient is taken to the postanesthesia care
unit for postoperative recovery.

Closure

We have the largest experience treating patients with sagittal
synostosis since we began operating in 1996 and presented
the first reported cases in 1998.14 As expected, the majority of the patients are male (318; 73.2%), whereas females
(117) make up 26.8% of the total group of 435 patients.
The median age at time of surgery is 3.6 months, with a
range between 2 weeks and 9 months of age. All data is
collected prospectively by a research nurse and values are
discussed and agreed upon with the anesthesia team. The
median blood loss is 25 mL, ranging between 5 mL and 150
mL for the entire group. A total of four patients have been
given blood transfusions during surgery and most of those
cases were done at the beginning of the series. After surgery
and during the first 24 hours, a total of 25 patients required
blood transfusions, thus making a 5.7% postoperative

Prior to skin/galeal closure, one last quick inspection of
the surgical field is undertaken with an inverted 30-degree
endoscope (Fig. 8.25). The vertex dura, sagittal sinus, lateral
and wedge osteotomies, and the dura under the osteotomies are visualized. A Valsalva maneuver is used to look for
cerebrospinal fluid leak or further bleeding. Surgiflo and/
or liquid thrombin is laid on the surgical field. Bupivacaine
(0.25%) with epinephrine (1:250,000) is injected into the
surgical incisions at 1 mL/kg. Additionally, bilateral supraorbital and greater occipital nerve blocks are done with the
local anesthetic. The galea is closed with interrupted 4-0
Monocryl sutures. The head is placed in the Trendelenburg
position and the scalp/hair is washed with baby shampoo

transfusion rate. We have only recently transfused one
patient during the last 9 years. None of the postoperative
transfusions were done urgently (i.e., “patient crashing”)
but were done out of precaution and particularly because
a large number of our patients come from out of state or
out of country. The overall transfusion rate for our sagittal synostosis is currently at 6.6%, which compares much
more favorably to the transfusion rates for calvarial vault
remodeling surgery. Ninety-nine percent of the patients
were discharged the morning after and none have had to
be readmitted for any reason within a week of discharge
from the hospital. The median operative time (skin-to-skin)
is 53 minutes. As previously stated, the patients are fitted
with custom-made cranial orthosis on the fifth postoperative day and a wear schedule, which is increased quickly to
23 hours a day, is instituted. The helmets are worn until the
child reaches 18 months of age. Cranial anthropometry and
standard photographs are taken at all visits, which, at minimum, take place at 2 months, 6 months, 12 months and 18
months. Thereafter, an annual or biannual follow-up visit is
done. Short- and long-term follow-up results and outcomes
are seen in Clinical Cases 1 to 5, Figs. 8.26–8.30.

Conclusion
We have published and presented long-term follow-up and
outcomes in patients diagnosed with sagittal craniosynostosis at an early age and treated with endoscopic assisted
techniques. Our results speak for themselves. Excellent
short- and long-term outcomes can be obtained without
subjecting the infant to the very invasive and traumatic
nature of traditional cranial vault remodeling procedures.
We have fully achieved our original goals of (1) decreasing
blood loss; (2) minimizing blood loss; (3) decreasing the
need and rate of blood transfusions; (4) decreasing in-hospital length of stay; (5) decreasing pain, trauma, and discomfort to the patient; (6) improving long-term outcomes and
results; and (7) decreasing costs. These procedures do not
require the use of stabilizing hardware, which is very expensive, particularly for developing countries. The management of an infant presenting with craniosynostosis should
be done early and with the described techniques. At a very
minimum, treating craniofacial centers should disclose to
the parents this surgical option and refer patients to centers
that do, if they choose not to provide this option in their
surgical armamentarium.

Craniosynostosis
DAVID F. JIMENEZ, MD, FACS

Introduction
Unilateral nonsyndromic coronal craniosynostosis is a challenging condition for the treating surgical team given all of
the deformational changes associated with this condition.
Unlike other single suture closures, unicoronal stenosis
leads to a combination of skull base, calvarial, and facial
deformities. It is a well-known fact that the phenotypic
presentation includes the following prominent features: (1)
ipsilateral vertical dystopia (Fig. 9.1); (2) ipsilateral proptosis (Fig. 9.2); (3) ipsilateral frontal plagiocephaly (Fig. 9.3);
(4) contralateral nasal tip deviation (Fig. 9.4); (5) ipsilateral exotropia (Fig. 9.5); (6) cranial scoliosis (Fig. 9.6); and
(7) strabismus. As the patients get older, correction of all of

• Fig. 9.1 Seven-month-old female with left coronal synostosis demon-

strates misalignment of the left orbital horizontal axis (green line) and
right orbital horizontal axis (red line).

these deformities becomes increasingly more difficult to do.
Consequently, the sooner that the problem is addressed, the
sooner the correction can take place. As stated in a previous Chapter 3, the timing of surgical intervention has been
an issue of debate amongst treating surgeons for several
decades. The balance of operating early or ultra-early needs
to be balanced with the significant detrimental effects of a
large and complex surgery on a young infant. The traditional
approach to these cases is to proceed with bifrontal craniotomies, orbital frontal bandeau advancement, and forward
movement of the affected side (fronto-orbital advancement
[FOA]). The recommendation is to wait until the patient is
9 to 12 months of age. By then the orbital, nasal, and skull
base deformities are fully set in and nearly impossible to
correct, particularly the vertical dystopia. Literature analysis
supports the fact that FOA is not really successful in correcting astigmatism, strabismus, or amblyopia and in cases,
can aggravate the condition iatrogenically.1 Furthermore, it
is commonly found in surgical reports that there is relapse
of the frontal bar, frontal craniotomies, and temporal hollowing.2–4 This fact has been clearly and elegantly shown
by McCarthy in his long-term outcome reports5 presented
in the plastic surgery literature. Given our success treating
sagittal synostosis with endoscopic-assisted, minimally invasive surgery, we began to modify and develop techniques to
release the stenosed suture at an extra early age with the goal
of allowing the brain to grow along normal vector forces
and correct the aforementioned deformities. As originally
theorized, we were very pleasantly surprised to see the rapid
correction of the congenitally related malformations.6–10 Of
utmost importance is the rebalancing of the dystopia associated with coronal closure. We noticed that as the brain
continued to grow along genetically predetermined vector
lines following sutural release, the entire bony orbital complex began to move inferiorly and anteriorly. This eventually led to eye realignment along the horizontal axis, thereby
correcting the vertical dystopia. Also evident was the slow
but continued realignment of the deviated nasal bone to
the midline. As the orbit repositioned in normal place, the

exotropia and strabismus also corrected. More details on
this correction are provided in the ophthalmic Chapter 7.
The last deformity to typically correct is the ipsilateral frontal plagiocephaly. This chapter will detail the techniques for
performing this procedural release in young infants.

Positioning
Following endotracheal intubation and placement of the
intravenous lines, a precordial doppler is secured on the

patient’s chest. The table is then turned 180 degrees away
from the anesthesia team and the patient’s head is placed
in a neutral position on a pediatric horseshoe head holder.
Disposable corneal protectors are carefully placed in each
eye using abundant amounts of an ophthalmic lubricating ointment. The head is then turned about 15 degrees
to the contralateral side of the synostosis and the entire
surgical field is draped in standard fashion (Fig. 9.7).
The stephanion (superior temporal line and coronal
suture junction) on the affected side is identified and the
entire face, including eyes and nose, are prepped with

monopolar set on cut mode at 15 watts. Deeper layers are opened

povidone-iodine scrub and paint solutions and appropriately allowed to dry for maximum effectiveness. A Mayo
stand is placed over the patient with all of the needed
instrumentation as shown in Chapter 4. Antibiotics
should be given intravenously 30 minutes prior to making the skin incision and a complete time out should be
done as well.

Incision
A small area of hair is clipped posterior to the stephanion
for placement of the incision (Fig. 9.8). The overall exposure and tissue dissection is relatively small and narrow, thus
requiring only a single small incision. To minimize blood
loss, a needle-tip Bovie unit is used to cut the dermis on the
cut mode at 15 watts (Fig. 9.9). Rapid movement minimizes
scarring and epidermal burn. Once epidermis is incised,
deeper dissection is done with the needle tip on coagulation
mode. The incision is typically 1.5 to 2 cm in length. As

bloodlessly. The same maneuver is done when developing the epidural
plane.

taken with the aid of the rhinoplasty lighted retractor (left side) and the
30-degree rigid endoscope and suction/dissector (right side). The area
of the anterior fontanelle is easily and rapidly reached.

described in Chapter 4, the subgaleal dissection is begun on
either side with the aid of an endoscopic lighted retractor.

Procedure
Subgaleal dissection is first directed towards the anterior
fontanelle using the monopolar and lighted retractor (Fig.
9.10). An endoscope can then be passed under the galea
to visualize the anterior fontanelle from above (Fig. 9.11).
The subgaleal dissection is then directed towards the area of
the pterion. Full release can then be accomplished by gently passing an insulated brain ribbon over the galea to reach

the pterion (Fig. 9.12). The needle tip is once again used to
elevate the scalp from the pericranium and temporalis muscle fascia. The rhinoplasty lighted retractor is very useful for
direct visualization or with the zero-degree endoscope. The
dissecting plane is developed to an area 1 or 2 cm anterior to
the ear and behind the pterion (Fig. 9.13). The option of dissecting the temporalis muscle from the underlying bone can
be done under direct visualization and with the aid of a #1
Penfield dissector. This maneuver elevates the temporalis off
the bone so that the osteotomy can be done without cutting
into the muscle fibers (Fig. 9.14). However, even when these
fibers are cut along their longitudinal axis, we have found no
negative residual effect after the surgery or subsequent to it.

Osteotomy
At the beginning of our series, we theorized that resection
of the entire stenosed suture was necessary for a successful

• Fig. 9.16 3-Dimensional computed tomography scan of a patient who

underwent an endoscopic craniectomy for right coronal synostosis at
another institution and who failed to improve and correct postoperative deformities. The scan shows an inappropriately placed osteotomy.
The bone resection failed to reach both the squamosal suture and the
anterior fontanelle. This failure is operator error and not the technique’s
failure.

• Fig. 9.15 The stenosed right coronal suture is shown by the solid blue
line extending from the fontanelle to the pterion. The scalp incision is
marked with the burr hole directly under it. The osteotomy is depicted
by the red dotted rectangular line extending from the fontanelle to the
squamosal suture (dotted line in front of right ear).

outcome. The procedure included placing the incision
directly over the suture and resecting both medial and lateral extensions. We made modifications to include making
a lateral frontal osteotomy over the orbital area, resecting
parts of the greater wing of the sphenoid bone, and osteotomizing the sphenofrontal suture. These maneuvers led
to unwanted and unnecessary blood loss (albeit still small
enough not to need transfusion). Seeing no significant
improvement to our earlier excellent results, we decided
to return to a more minimalistic approach. A decision was
made not to necessarily resect the actual suture but instead
to create an entire new osteotomy and extend it posterior to
the pterion (Fig. 9.15). The osteotomy width need not be

greater than 4 or 5 mm as the growing brain will widen the
osteotomy over time. Absolute care must be taken to assure
that the osteotomy reaches the squamosal suture. Failure to
do so leaves a band of bone connecting and attaching the
frontal and temporal bones. This bony connection prevents
the forward movement of the fronto-orbital complex and
deformity correction. When doing consultation for other
center’s failures, invariably it can be seen in the postoperative computed tomography (CT) scans that complete release
was not done by the operating surgeons (Fig. 9.16). The
osteotomy is begun with a small burr hole using a 7-mm
pediatric craniotome directly at the incision site. The round
end of a #1 Penfield dissector is used to gently free the dura
from the bone towards the anterior fontanelle (Fig. 9.17).
The Penfield dissector is advanced to the edge of the fontanelle, which is palpated with the nondominant hand over
the scalp. The osteotomy can be done with the Mayo scissors or rongeurs (Fig. 9.18). Endoscopic visualization of the
bony-dura interface is useful and can help minimize complications such as dural tears. The endoscope (30-degree)
also allows the surgeon to directly see the fibers of the anterior fontanelle (Fig. 9.19) assuring total medial release of
the frontal and parietal bones (Fig. 9.20). Once the medial
release has been achieved, bony hemostasis is done with the
suction electrocautery unit and Surgiflo. Attention is then
paid to dissection of the epidural plane between the burr
hole and the squamosal suture. The burr hole is enlarged
longitudinally with 5-mm Kerrison rongeurs for a distance
of 1 or 2 cm. This bony enlargement allows the insertion
of a 30-degree endoscope under the bone, which is then
advanced towards the ear (Fig. 9.21). The endoscope and
small suction are passed in tandem and the dura is separated

from the bone. Sweeping motion movements allow for visualization of the frontal dura, the greater wing of the sphenoid
bone (Fig. 9.22), and the point where the closed coronal
suture opens as it nears the pterion (Fig. 9.23). Further
epidural dissection is then undertaken posterior inferiorly
towards the squamosal suture (Fig. 9.24). Once the squamosal suture is reached, all of the room lights are shut down
and the endoscope tip is trans-illuminated and marked with
a skin marker. This mark is useful in knowing the location
of the suture and direction of the osteotomy (Fig. 9.25). The
osteotomy is then done with bony removal using the bone
cutting rongeurs until the squamosal suture is reached (Fig.
9.26). Osseous hemostasis of the area around the squamosal suture is accomplished by placing an insulated 7-French
(small) suction and delivering the coagulating energy with

• Fig. 9.20 The strip of bone is being removed after full release all the
way to the anterior fontanelle.

the suction cautery unit set at 60 watts. The connection is
made at the base of the suction (Fig. 9.27) and the energy
carefully delivered to the osteotomy site. Utmost care is
taken to prevent cauterization of either the scalp above or
the dura below. Another reason to make the osteotomy at a
different location than the coronal suture itself, is that it is
not uncommon for the stenosed suture to have a deep bony
ridge that extends deep and indents the underlying dura
(Fig. 9.28). If care is not taken and the cut is made directly

After adequately cauterizing the area around the squamosal
suture, the rest of the bone can be cauterized with the suction cautery unit. The head is placed in the Trendelenburg
position and the surgical field is copiously irrigated with

hematocrit is obtained, as well as an anteroposterior (AP)
and lateral skull radiograph (Fig. 9.30). Following extubation, the patient is transferred to the postanesthesia care
unit and then to the pediatric intensive care unit. Discharge
from the hospital takes place the following morning.

Results
Our series consists of a total of 140 patients with predominant preference for females (97) and less male penetrance
(43). The coronal cohort makes up 17% of our entire craniosynostosis group treated with endoscopic techniques.
The median age at time of surgery is 4.1 months (0.5 to 10
months). Blood loss is the lowest of all treated groups at a
median of 15 cc. There have been no patients who required
blood transfusions during surgery (0%) and only one was
transfused postoperatively for a 0.7% rate. As with all other
sutures, almost every patient was observed overnight and
discharged the following morning. The mean surgical time

• Fig. 9.28 A stenosed coronal suture in a 3-month-old girl showing

a deep underside ridge (B) which indents into the underlying dura (A).
Making an osteotomy in this area increases the risk of dural injury if not
properly visualized and dissected.

was 48 minutes with a range between 21 and 150 minutes.
Anthropometric analysis of our first 115 patients showed
marked improvement on most craniofacial deformities.
Fifty one percent of the patients had 100% correction of
the vertical dystopia, while two-thirds of the patients had
corrections greater than 80% (Figs. 9.31 and 9.32). This
compares significantly better to calvarial vault remodeling

(CVR) surgery done at a later age when minimal to no correction of the dystopia occurs. Another significant area of
correction occurs with the cranial scoliosis or sagittal plane
imbalance. The normal straight line between vertex-nasionmention becomes angulated and distorted at the nasion
(Figs. 9.33–9.36). In our series, 77% of the patients have
achieved 100% correction of this imbalance. Overall, our
long-term results are excellent and much better than any
result we ever had with the open CVR surgery.
The patients who presented with strabismus secondary to
orbital misalignment only need to have ocular muscle surgical correction 4.8% of the time. We believe that realignment
of the orbital bony housing contributed to realignment of
orbital musculature leading to improvement/correction of

• Fig. 9.32 Ten months after undergoing endoscopic craniectomy
release, the patient demonstrates that her left orbit has lowered and
lies along the exo-endocanthial axis of the right eye.

the strabismus as seen in Figs. 9.37–9.40. When performing
a CVR procedure, the primary goal is to do an FOA of the
recessed ipsilateral forehead and supraorbital rim. Careful
analysis of our patients using postoperative AP and lateral
radiographs has shown that early release of the stenosed
coronal suture allows the growing brain to do the FOA
internally and without the need for invasive and extensive
surgery. The results can be seen in Figs. 9.41–9.46 where the

size of the osteotomy is widened significantly from the original osteotomy. Settling of the orbit and correction of the
vertical dystopia has also been documented radiographically
(Figs. 9.47–9.51). The aforementioned correction has also
been documented anthropometrically with the Star Scanner
(Fig. 9.52).

The primary goal of postoperative helmet therapy, after
releasing the stenosed suture, is to create space for the ipsilateral frontal lobe to grow forward and inferiorly and mobilize
the frontal bone, supraorbital rim, and the orbital roof. Direct
contact is placed on the contralateral frontal bone in order
to hold it in place as the ipsilateral frontal bone advances
(Fig. 9.53.). Short- and long-term results can be seen in Figs.
9.54–9.59, corresponding to Clinical Cases 1 to 6.

Conclusion

bones, the brain (during its very rapid brain growth phase)
is allowed to correct the number of congenital deformities.
These deformities cannot be corrected by simply moving
the frontal bone and orbital rim forward as done with a
CVR at later ages. Our extensive anthropometric, radiographic, and photographic data support our assertions. This
surgical option should be given to all parents of children
with unicoronal synostosis as part of a complete informed
consent.

The complex nature of treating unilateral, nonsyndromic
unicoronal craniosynostosis has been significantly simplified with the introduction of endoscopic, minimally invasive sutural release in young infants. The procedure itself is
simple and relatively easy to perform. It is associated with
the least amount of blood loss, transfusion needs, and surgical exposure. By disconnecting the fused frontal and parietal
