Osteotomies
If the anterior fontanelle is still open it can be carefully dissected so that a Kerrison rongeur can be used to
begin the metopic osteotomy. A 30-degree endoscope is
advanced under the bone down to the nasion and a 4- to
5-mm craniectomy developed so that both frontal bones
are released. If the fontanelle is closed, a 7-mm burr hole
can be made with a pediatric perforator for an epidural
access and dissection. At the coronal incision, a pediatric
perforator is used to make a burr hole. The osteotomy is
then taken medially to reach the anterior fontanelle and
join with the mid frontal osteotomy used to release the
metopic suture. Epidural dissection is directed towards
the pterion and then the osteotomy completed so that it
reaches the squamosal suture and pterion. These maneuvers ultimately lead to release of both fused frontal bones
as well as release of the ipsilateral frontal bone from the
parietal and temporal bones on the side of the stenosed
coronal suture (Fig. 14.31). Closure is done as previously
described in the coronal chapter (Chapter 9). Cranial
orthotic therapy is instituted postoperatively to guide the
brain growth in the proper direction and correct the preoperative deformities.

Results
There were four patients, two males and two females. The
mean age at time of surgery was 3.1 months and the median

were 2.9 months. The mean estimated blood loss was 15
mL. There were no intraoperative or postoperative blood
transfusions. The mean surgical time was 77 minutes. All
patients were discharged on POD 1. There were no complications. Correction of the preoperative deformities can be
seen in Fig. 14.32.

Bilateral Lambdoid Synostosis
It is estimated that 15% of lambdoid synostosis cases are
of the bilateral type3; bilambdoid synostosis accounted for
13.4% in our series, confirming pervious reports. Increased
intracranial pressure has been reported in 86% of the cases.4
Accordingly, these patients tend to be irritable and fussy
which can be accounted by the elevated intracranial pressure. The growth of the cerebellum is significantly higher
than that of the cerebrum during the first year of life. Closure of both sutures leads to marked volume decrease of the
posterior fossa and, subsequently, Chiari I malformations
have been reported but the true incidence is unknown.
Overall, there is a similar phenotypic presentation in all of
the patients and consists, as would be expected, of bilateral
frontal bossing and turricephaly (Fig. 14.33) with elongation of the vertex to accommodate for the restriction of
brain growth posteriorly (Fig. 14.34). The normal biparietal eminences and calvarial widening are absent. Instead,
the patient’s parieto-occipital bones are slanted and narrow (Fig. 14.35) and when viewed from above, the occiput
takes a V-shape. The posterior fossa’s occipital bone becomes
indented in the midline (Fig. 14.36) and aligns more in the
axial plane. Current treatment protocols advocate waiting
until patient is 1 to 12 months before proceeding with a

bioccipital advancement.5 The concept is borrowed from

bifrontal-orbital advancement where a bandeau is harvested
and rounded out to reshape the occiput. Large parietal bone
flaps are harvested and reshaped (as done in bifrontal cases)
and then attached to the bandeau to produce overcorrection and increase cranial vault volume posteriorly. These
surgeries are particularly challenging and difficult to execute
because the major draining venous sinuses are all under the
bones that require removal and are under pressure. The confluence of sinus (sagittal, straight, and transverse sinuses) is
compressed and difficult to dissect. Additionally, the transverse and sigmoid sinuses need to be dissected. Even the

most minor of tears can lead to significant blood losses. In
order to avoid these challenges and possible complications,
we decided to apply the aforementioned endoscopic craniosynostosis principles to treating this specific condition and
have done so with great success.

Position
The patient is placed fully prone with the head resting on
a cerebellar horseshoe headrest, making sure that the eyes
are not under any pressure from the headrest (Fig. 14.37).

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 14

• Fig. 14.37 The patient is placed fully prone on a cerebellar horseshoe,
taking care to protect the eyes from undue pressure. This position
gives easy access to both asterion and the lambda.

Multiple Suture Nonsyndromic Craniosynostosis

205

• Fig. 14.39 To assist in proper localization of the cranial landmarks,
metal localizing pellets are used with anteroposterior and lateral skull
radiographs.

Osteotomies
A

B

C

• Fig. 14.38 The location of the incision is denoted by the solid lines at
the lambda and both asterion.

This position allows easy and direct access to the lambda
and both asterion. As with all other cases, a precordial Doppler is used to monitor for venous air emboli.

Incisions
The goal is to have access to the lambda and both asterions. The midline lambda incision is placed directly over the
lambda and measures 2 to 3 cm and is transversely placed.
The other incisions are smaller (1–1.5 cm) and are placed
at or near each asterion (Fig. 14.38). As described in the
unilateral chapter (Chapter 13), localizing metal pellets can
be used with fluoroscopy to localize these landmarks accurately (Fig. 14.39). This set up allows access for resection
and release of both lambdoid sutures.

Burr holes 7 mm-wide are placed on either side of the
sagittal suture at the level of the lambda. The osteotomy
is made across the midline with Kerrison rongeurs after
an epidural plane is safely developed with the aid of a #1
Penfield dissector. Similar size burr holes are placed immediately medial to the asterion on each side. The Penfield
dissector is used to free the dura towards the lambda incision. Likewise, an epidural plane dissection is begun at the
lambda and directed towards the asterion. Kerrisons are
used to elongate and enlarge the burr hole, at the lambda,
towards the asterion. This maneuver allows placement of a
rigid 30-degree endoscope under the bone. The endoscope
is advanced visualizing the stenosed lambdoid suture,
towards the asterion burr hole and reaching it. Once the
dura has been fully and safely dissected from the overlying bone, the osteotomy can be made with either bone
cutting scissors or with a bone cutting rongeur, in a piecemeal fashion until both burr holes are in communication.
The same procedure is then done on the contralateral side,
thereby releasing the entire occipital complex from both
parietal bones (Fig. 14.40). Thermostasis is obtained with
electrocautery and Surgiflo. As with all other sutures, the
patients are placed in helmets that will allow for occipital
expansion, advancement, and normalization of the occipitoparietal complex.

Results
There were five patients, two males and three females. The
mean age at time of surgery was 4.1 months. The mean
estimated blood loss was 25 mL. There were no intraoperative or postoperative blood transfusions. The mean surgical time was 64 minutes. All patients were discharged on
POD 1. There were no complications. All patients wore a

booksmedicos.org

206 C HA P T ER 1 4

Multiple Suture Nonsyndromic Craniosynostosis

set of cranial orthoses which led to marked and significant
improvements over time. An example of the results can be
seen in a 2-month-old male in Figs. 14.41–14.43.

Bilambdoid and Sagittal Synostosis

• Fig. 14.40 The craniectomies to release the stenosed lambdoid
sutures are depicted in the shaded green area.

A

It has been our experience that most patients with bilateral lambdoid synostosis also have involvement of the sagittal suture in an area near the lambda. This area of sagittal
synostosis is easily addressed while removing both lambdoid sutures by extending the osteotomy several centimeters towards the vertex until open sagittal is encountered.
However, there is another subgroup of bilateral lambdoid
synostosis patients who have stenosis of the entire sagittal
suture and present with the classical “Mercedes-Benz” pattern. Somewhat similar to the bilambdoid synostosis, these
patients present with varying degrees of anterior turricephaly and forehead prominence and widening, brachycephaly,
reduced posterior cranial vault height and width, widening
of the posterior skull base, and sharp angulation and midline indentation. Additionally, seen are biparietal narrowing, occipital squama invagination resulting in a step-like

B

C

• Fig. 14.41 (A–C) Preoperative views of a two-month-old male prior to undergoing endoscopic surgical
sutural release.

A

B

Incisions
A lambda incision and bilateral asterion incisions are made
as previously described. In addition, a fourth incision is
made at or near the anterior fontanelle (Fig. 14.50). This
combination allows access for resection of a wide vertex craniectomy and anterior bilateral wedge osteotomies, as well
as resection of both lambdoid sutures (Fig. 14.51).

Osteotomies
Following subgaleal dissection of the vertex and over the
affected lambdoid sutures, burr holes are made as previously described. Anterior and posterior midline transverse
osteotomies are made with Kerrison rongeurs across the
midline sagittal suture and sinus. Following epidural plane
endoscopic dissection, a wide vertex craniectomy is done
to release the stenosed sagittal suture. Bilateral frontal
wedge osteotomies are made behind the coronal sutures.
Bilateral asterion burr holes are made and then the endoscopic-assisted lambdoid craniectomies are finished, thereby
releasing all of the stenosed sutures (Fig. 14.52).

Postoperative Cranial
Orthotic Therapy
DEANNA J. FISH, CPO AND CHRISTINA HINTON, CP

Introduction to Cranial Remolding
Programs
Cranial remolding orthoses (CROs) were first described in
the treatment of deformational plagiocephaly in 1979.1–3
The underlying principle of “directed growth” is accomplished by designing the internal surface of the orthosis to
maintain total contact over the prominent areas and provide
void spaces over the flattened areas of the infant’s developing
skull. With continued brain and skull growth, asymmetrical and disproportionate head shapes are molded towards
normocephaly within the orthosis. Based on this concept of
directed growth, early success with the use of CROs for the
postoperative management of craniosynostosis was reported
by Persing et al. (1986) after cranial vault reconstruction
and Jimenez & Barone (1998) after endoscopic removal
of the affected suture(s). Postoperative CROs are designed
to enhance the surgical procedure with continued reshaping of the deviated skull4–7 and prevention of secondary
deformity.8
Currently, postoperative CROs are recommended as
standard treatment and adjuncts to endoscopic4,6,9–20 and
some open cranial vault surgical procedures.21 Successful outcomes with CROs are dependent on many factors
including but not limited to:
• age of the infant,
• remaining growth of brain and skull,
• affected suture(s),
• specific clinical indications and contraindications,
• modifications and design of the cranial orthosis,
• ongoing adjustments to optimize fit and function of the
orthosis,
• need for consecutive CROs,
• caregiver compliance with the wearing schedule,
• potential complications,
• skills of the cranial orthotist,
• duration of the orthotic treatment program, and
• communication and collaboration of the entire medical
team.

Structured clinical follow-up and precise modifications to
consecutive orthoses are provided over a strategic period of
time.18 Ultimately, successful postoperative CRO programs
are provided by experienced and trained cranial orthotists
working in partnership with the surgical team.

Influence of Infant Age at Time of Surgery
Endoscopic procedures may be considered for infants up to
6 months of age; however, the best outcomes are obtained
prior to 3 months of age.9,11,22 As the function of the CRO
is dependent on growth, the normal patterns of brain and
skull growth must be accounted for throughout the entire
postoperative orthotic remolding program. Up to 12 cm of
expansion in cranial circumference is experienced during the
first year after birth.10 Specifically, infant growth patterns
predict 2 cm per month during the first 3 months of age; 1
cm per month from 4 to 6 months of age; and 0.5 cm per
month from 6 to 12 months of age. Surgical corrections and
focused orthotic treatment programs capture critical magnitudes of growth in the early months that are unattainable at
later ages. Simple mathematics dictate that a severe deformity treated at 3 months of age might reach normocephalic
parameters within the subsequent 3 months, whereas the initiation of an orthotic remolding program at 8 months of age
may require orthotic treatment for at least 6 to 8 months to
obtain similar outcomes. Postoperative CRO programs after
early endoscopic release continue until at least 12 months
of age in order to establish normocephaly, to overcorrect
specific skull dimensions in some cases, and/or to reduce
the risk of recurrence of the deformity.1,4,6,8–10,13–15,19,23–34
Consecutive orthoses may be necessary to effectively direct
and manage the anticipated 6 cm or more of circumferential
cranial growth while still maintaining accurate fit and effective function of the CRO.11 Fig. 15.1 summarizes the influence of infant age relative to the goals of each phase of the
postoperative CRO program18 and outlines cranial growth
patterns relative to the use of consecutive CROs that will be
discussed at a later point.

• Fig. 15.1 Influence of infant age on postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) remolding programs.
(Courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

As a community of bones, each skull plate contacts adjacent anatomical structures. CROs effect change directly to the
neurocranium with indirect changes to the viscerocranium.
Primary coverage areas include the frontal, temporal, sphenoid, parietal, and occipital bones; metopic, sagittal, coronal,
lambdoid, and squamous sutures; and anterior, posterior,
anterolateral, and posterolateral fontanelles. The physiological function, limitations, deviations, and malformations of
these anatomical structures must be considered in the orthotic
design process to determine the modification strategies and
guide the fitting techniques (Fig. 15.2). Early surgical correction allows greater postoperative skull growth to produce a
symmetrical and proportional neurocranium. Delayed treatments limit the growth opportunities that a symmetrical and
proportional skull may have on facial symmetry, proportion,
and overall development. The clinical rationale for early and
sustained postoperative orthotic treatment programs has been
well-established.18,25,26,35

• Fig. 15.2 Infant wearing postoperative cranial remolding orthosis
(CRO) with specific modifications and fit to address metopic synostosis. (Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

Postoperative CRO Indications/
Recommendations
There is an abundance of medical literature to support the use
of CROs after endoscopic release to maintain and improve the
surgical correction, direct continued skull shape improvements

over time, and also prevent secondary deformities. Many authors
report the use of postoperative CROs as a critical component
to the endoscopic surgical approach.4–14,20,21,24–26,34,36–39 Postoperative CROs are used less frequently in cases of cranial vault
reconstruction surgeries21 when the surgical procedure itself

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 15

is used to remodel the overall shape of the skull. CROs are
considered in these cases for severe involvements and also to
address the deformities developed from external forces.
Initial improvement in the overall shape of the skull may
occur immediately after endoscopic procedures as a result
of releasing the fused suture, particularly in sagittal cases.
CROs serve to capture and maintain the initial surgical correction, and then continue to direct improvements in symmetry, proportion, and overall shape towards normocephaly.
Most CROs are scanned, fabricated, and fitted within 7 to
10 days of surgery, allowing for some dissipation of the subgaleal swelling to ensure an optimal initial fit and function
of the orthosis.4,10,12,14,15,18,22–24,26,29–34 The cranial orthotist and craniofacial team must collaborate on all aspects of
the postoperative care programs, while also reinforcing and
supporting the caregivers’ commitment to postoperative
treatment strategies.

CRO Contraindications
The medical literature lacks professional consensus regarding the optimal indications, timing, and procedures for
endoscopic release.37 Similar concerns may also be applied
to the postoperative orthotic treatment program. CROs vary
in design, materials, manufacturing, clinical techniques, fit,
function, and, ultimately, clinical outcomes. Requirements
for a successful orthotic treatment program include a skilled
and experienced cranial orthotist with specialized training
in postoperative cranial remolding programs. The family
must be committed to consistent clinical follow-up for strategic adjustments to the orthosis over an extended course
of treatment.14 CRO remolding programs for postoperative
cases rely on brain and skull growth and are therefore most
effective when orthotic programs are completed within the
first 12 months after birth. Without these key components,
the success of endoscopic approaches will be limited, and
other surgical procedures might be considered.9

Postoperative CRO Program
An experienced cranial orthotist is a critical member of the
craniofacial team.3,8,9,12,38 Regular communication between
the cranial orthotist and surgical team is necessary14,23 as
many private orthotic clinics operate outside of hospital
communities. Cranial clinicians pursue advanced education
for both deformational and postoperative cranial remolding
concepts and utilize innovative scanning devices along with
a variety of Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-cleared
orthotic designs. These orthotic specialists should be provided with necessary surgical details (e.g., strip craniectomy,
wedge osteotomies or barrel staves, cranial springs, etc.) to
conceptualize the flexibility of the infant’s skull after surgery
to enhance orthosis-directed growth opportunities. Individual patient presentations, surgical procedures, and surgeon
preferences will impact the modifications to the head model,
orthotic material and design selections, initial and ongoing corrections anticipated, and overall orthotic treatment

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

219

• Fig. 15.3 Processing of infant head model and clinical specifications prior to fabrication of postoperative cranial remolding orthosis
(CRO). (Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

objectives. Processing of infant head model and clinical specifications prior to fabrication of postoperative CROs need to
be carefully planned and executed (Fig. 15.3). Many cranial
orthotists offer preoperative evaluations, family education,
anthropometric measurements, scanning procedures, and
detailed reports for the craniofacial team. Preoperative visits
serve to increase the family’s understanding of the overall
orthotic treatment program, as well as the importance of the
specific daily tasks needed to maintain proper hygiene of the
orthosis for their infant. Families will be returning to the
cranial orthotist for structured follow-up, anthropometric
measurements, modifications to the CRO, additional scanning procedures, continued education about the orthosis,
and general support to successfully complete the treatment
program with optimal results. Surgical teams should expect
timely reports on head shape changes (Fig. 15.4) as the
collective attention to the orthotic treatment processes by
the entire team reduces the need for additional corrective
procedures.19 Postoperative orthotic protocols and guidelines should be developed to address specific patient profiles, surgical procedures, medical team protocols, orthotic
scanning and fitting routines, orthotic design features, and
clinical follow-up schedules that best support the needs of
the craniofacial team. As an example, Fig. 15.4 shows the
preoperative scan and anthropometric measurements which
are compared to the 4- and 9-months postoperative scans
and reflect a persistent change in the cephalic ratio.

CRO Design Specifications
Postoperative CROs are specific-to-patient, class II medical
devices requiring 510(k) submission and FDA clearance to
ensure safety and efficacy of this time-sensitive medical treatment program. Just as surgical techniques vary relative to suture
involvement, design features of postoperative CROs are specific to the suture involvement and clinical techniques identified by the medical team.18,25,26,40 CROs are labor-intensive
orthopedic devices that must be specifically and individually
fitted to each child within a few days after the postoperative
scanning procedures. One of the primary advantages of a
CRO is the ability to continuously mold the head in both
linear and volumetric dimensions. This is accomplished by

220 C HA P T ER 1 5

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

• Fig. 15.4 Preoperative (left), 4 months (middle), and 9 months (right) postoperative head scans show

increased cephalic ratio (CR) and consistent normalization of head shape over time. (Courtesy Dr. David
F. Jimenez.)

a thorough evaluation of the initial head shape, understanding of surgical procedures, planned growth strategies, model
rectification specifications, product and design selections, and
timely fitting of the orthosis after surgery.

Scanning Procedures
Cranial clinicians now have access to a variety of portable
and stationary scanners that utilize eye-safe lasers, photographs, videography, and other methods to capture an
accurate digital image of the infant’s head (Fig. 15.5). Scans
are often acquired preoperatively (for family education and
end of treatment outcome comparisons), immediately after
surgery (to document surgical correction and use for fabrication), and then throughout the course of the orthotic treatment program to track efficacy of the orthotic strategy.10,12,34
Scanning sessions are often supplemented with manual
measurements by various clinical team members to confirm
appropriately directed growth, although it is important to
keep in mind that manual and scanner measurements will
vary slightly depending upon the clinical technique and measurement tools used (Fig. 15.6). All scanning technologies
used for CROs must also pass safety and accuracy standards
as part of the FDA 510(k) submission and clearance process.

specifications have continued to evolve since their introduction in the 1980s.41 Once the affected suture is removed,

postoperative CROs function similarly to those designed
for deformational skull involvements in that they resist and
promote skull growth in deliberate directions. Many initial
postoperative designs are made of a clear plastic material,
such as Surlyn, to allow visual inspection of the suture sites
as well as direct viewing of the contact and void areas within
the orthosis. Foam padding is added specific to suture
involvement to create a firm initial resistance to prominent
areas and guide the continued growth of the postoperative
head shape. Design considerations for various head shapes
may also include ease of donning and doffing by caregivers,
corrective pressures with pads or strapping configurations,
stability and purchase on the head to resist rotation and tipping, maintenance of a hygienic environment, ventilation
holes for improved air flow, and other factors unique to the
infant, surgical procedure, and care/caregiver environments
(Figs. 15.7).

Trim Lines
CRO trim lines are very important to capture specific
areas of the neurocranium to guide growth and to assist
in stabilizing the orthosis on the child’s head. Basic trim
lines and contours of the CRO extend anteriorly to the
eyebrows, posteriorly into the suboccipital region, laterally over the temporal bone, and distally under the mastoid areas. The ear openings must not impinge on the
ears; the temporal extensions must not limit peripheral
vision; and the suboccipital region must not discourage
neck extension, lateral tilt or rotation. Trim line variations are common and may occur specific to the surgical
procedure, at the request of the surgeon, and/or based
upon the clinical strategies of the cranial orthotist. In

• Fig. 15.8 Basic postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) trim

lines (lateral view) can be further trimmed and modified as needed.
(Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

some cases, trim lines may be left slightly longer to
account for anticipated growth and to extend the use of
the orthosis for as long as possible (Fig. 15.8).

Side-Opening, Bivalve and Living Hinge
Designs
Side-opening, bivalve (i.e., clamshell), and living hinge
designs (Fig. 15.9) are recommended based upon the

booksmedicos.org

222 C HA P T ER 1 5

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

Surlyn designs for the initial postoperative orthosis and
may transition to a foam lined CRO for consecutive designs
in order to extend the use of each orthosis over a longer
period of time. These recommendations are discussed by the
medical team based upon the individual patient and family needs. Continued advancements in three-dimensional
printing capabilities may soon bring new design options
for postoperative treatment programs in the United States
pending 510(k) submission and FDA clearance.
• Fig. 15.9 Examples of side-opening (left), bivalve (middle), and living
hinge (right) postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) designs.
(Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

specific skull deformity.18,26 Side-opening designs are
often used for coronal and lambdoid suture involvements
to address frontal and parietal deformations, as well as
overall symmetry and proportion. The proximal openings
in side-opening band designs may vary in size, shape, and
location to further address unique head shape anomalies.
Bivalve and living hinge designs are often recommended
for sagittal and metopic suture releases to specifically resist
growth in the anterior-posterior dimension while promoting lateral expansion in the temporal and parietal regions.
Bivalve and living hinge designs are also recommended to
maintain contact over the open sagittal suture and when
the surgeon prefers total contact over the proximal parietal
regions.

Materials
Materials used in postoperative CRO designs are detailed in
the 510(k) applications submitted to obtain FDA clearance
for manufacturing and marketing of the orthoses. Some
CRO designs are foam-lined with up to ½-inch inner foam
liner to allow for strategic removal of material over time
to direct and accommodate substantial three-dimensional
head growth. The foam lining may be made up of single
or multiple layers, and the outer shell is often a copolymer
or similar thermoplastic that is either draped or bubble/
blister formed over the modified head shape. The cranial
orthotist will enhance the fit and function of the orthosis
over time with the addition or removal of the foam lining. Reston foam is commonly used to provide initial stabilization of the orthosis, resist tipping and rotation, and
compress easily with rapid cranial growth. Unlined CROs
are often fabricated with Surlyn (or similar) thermoplastic
materials.18,25,26 Smaller pads are added relative to the specific suture involvement and a relatively large portion of the
orthosis remains unlined. Examples of various padding configurations for common sutural involvements are shown in
Fig. 15.10. These configurations provide a starting point for
the skull remolding process, and the cranial orthotist continues to modify the fit with the strategic addition and/or
removal of the foam material over time. Considerable modifications are available with skilled heating and stretching of
the Surlyn headband. Orthotists and surgeons often prefer

Modifications
Modifications to the positive model during the manufacturing process may be applied by computer-aided design
(CAD) or manual methods (Fig. 15.11) and are based upon
the infant’s age, anticipated skull growth, suture involvement, specific head shape, and clinical technique preferred
by the surgeon and cranial orthotist. These modifications may be minimal or major in added dimension, and
are designed to improve symmetry, proportion, sloping,
and overall shape of the infant’s skull. Clinical techniques
vary in the degree of modification (i.e., looseness or snugness of initial fit) and are coupled with the strategic addition of pads, removal of inner foam lining material, and/or
heated expansion of the outer plastic shell throughout the
course of treatment. After fabrication of the orthosis, the
cranial orthotist continues to make critical modifications to
the inner surface, overall contours, and trim lines to further modify skull growth dimensions. These modifications
require a structured follow-up program to anticipate cranial
growth opportunities. These directed growth strategies and
adjustments are supplemented by manual measurements at
each visit and regular scanning procedures to assess global
changes in head shape dimensions.9,12,32

Fabrication and Manufacturing
Rules and regulations for class II medical devices are constantly changing. It is important that allied healthcare professionals ensure that all patients receive FDA-cleared class
II medical devices to assure the safety and effectiveness of
the postoperative CROs and scanning systems. FDA clearance specifically addresses the materials, design, quality controls, and fabrication of all CROs (Fig. 15.12A–D); FDA
labeling requires specific information relative to the orthotic
treatment programs be provided with the orthosis. Cranial
orthotists will share the caregiver instructions, wearing
schedule, and cleaning information with the parents during
delivery appointments and continue to revisit these topics as
needed throughout the treatment program. At times, it will
be necessary to provide additional documentation for the
fabrication of the postoperative cranial orthosis to maintain
compliance with FDA regulations. In these cases, a copy
of the prescription and letter of medical necessity may be
requested and retained by the cranial orthotist and CRO
manufacturer.

Specific Suture Involvements: Sagittal,
Metopic, and Coronal
Each infant’s suture involvement and surgical correction
must be evaluated individually as it relates to the orthotic
design and specifications (Fig. 15.13). Most craniofacial
teams prefer the immediate postoperative orthotic design
to focus on the correction of the major skull anomalies
secondary to the synostosis, and lesser deformational concerns will be addressed in consecutive orthotic designs. The
orthotic design and clinical technique used to treat postoperative cases vary slightly from deformational cases in that
slight compression is used in strategic manners to prompt
rapid skull correction, especially in the first weeks with
the initial postoperative CRO. This is possible in younger
patients once the affected suture has been removed and
the infant’s skull presents with significant flexibility and
malleability. The foundational element of postoperative
orthotic treatment programs is brain and skull growth, and
therefore the age of the infant at the time of surgery and
immediate initiation of the orthotic treatment program are
critical to successful outcomes.8,11,23,25,26,31,38 In the first
6 months of life, surgical and orthotic treatments capitalize on an extremely malleable skull, rapid brain and skull
growth (i.e., up to 9 cm of circumferential growth in total),
and three-dimensional modeling opportunities. An additional 3 cm of circumferential cranial growth is directed
over the next 6 months to achieve and maintain correction.
General orthotic considerations for the most common
forms of craniosynostosis and postoperative cranial designs
are detailed here.

Sagittal Synostosis
Sagittal synostosis produces a scaphocephalic head shape
with significant parietal narrowing and frontal and occipital
bossing. Endoscopic removal of the affected sagittal suture
produces immediate increases in cranial width secondary
to removal of the sutural growth restriction. The custom
CRO is specifically designed to overcorrect the cephalic
ratio (CR) as quickly as possible to approach normocephaly (see phases I and II, Fig. 15.1). This is accomplished
by restricting growth in the anterior-posterior direction and
allowing growth in the lateral dimension with biparietal
expansion.17,29,38,42 Orthotic design specifications include
frontal and occipital padding configurations and full contact over of the top of the head to enhance corrective efforts
(Fig. 15.14). The design and goal of the initial CRO are to
increase the cephalic index by approximately 10% in the
first 6 to 8 weeks after fitting. Additional increases in the
CR and corrections to the lateral and/or posterior sloping
will occur with strategic orthotic design and adjustments
over the entire course of the treatment program. Overcorrection of the CR is not uncommon as a slight decrease in
CR is often noted after discontinuation of the remolding
orthosis (see phase III, Fig. 15.1).

Metopic Synostosis
Removal of the metopic suture allows orthotic remolding
efforts to create midline sagittal and posterior parietal control
to direct growth into fronto-orbital and anterior parietal expansion.6,18,24,42,43 Mild forms of deformational plagiocephaly

booksmedicos.org

B
• Fig. 15.11 (A and B) Model modifications may be applied by computer-assisted design (CAD) (top photo)
or manual (bottom photo) methods.

often accompany this involvement and are addressed after
an initial emphasis on the primary correction in the frontal
region. Again, specific padding configurations within the
orthosis focus cranial growth into the anterior quadrants
while maintaining the anterior-posterior and posterior-lateral
dimensions (Fig. 15.15). The anterior trim line in metopic
cases extends distally between the eyebrows to maintain contact over the open suture. Consecutive orthoses continue to
remold the affected skull towards normocephaly, with orthotic
treatment often continued until about 12 months of age.

Unicoronal Synostosis
Skull deformities associated with unicoronal synostosis include ipsilateral frontal-parietal flattening and orbit

elevation, along with deviation of the nasal root toward the
affected side. The postoperative orthotic design is aimed at
aggressively holding the bossed forehead while overcorrecting with a large void over the area of the affected anterior
quadrant. In Fig. 15.16, the red line shows the initial head
shape, whereas the blue line shows initial modification
corrections for frontal asymmetry, parietal narrowing and
sloping, and posterior flattening. Anterior deviations are
addressed first with directed brain and skull growth; posterior deformational asymmetries are addressed afterward.
However, some posterior expansion should be accommodated for very young infants when substantial circumferential increase (e.g., 2 cm) is expected within the first few
weeks. This rapid growth cannot be fully contained or
directed anteriorly and will result in the patient outgrowing

the orthosis quickly unless adjustments to the orthosis are
made. These adjustments must accommodate gradual relief
in the lateral and posterior dimensions. Frontal corrections
are obtained with directed brain growth and a sustained
orthotic treatment program up to 12 months of age.37,42

Bilateral Coronal and Bilateral Lambdoid
Synostoses
Brachycephalic deformities require restriction of lateral skull
growth with directed anterior-posterior growth to decrease
the cephalic ratio. Bicoronal involvements also require

symmetrically directed growth in the frontal regions, and
bilateral lambdoid presentations require correction to both
anterior and posterior asymmetries. As with all deformations, linear measurements are indicative of change;
however, ongoing volume assessments better reflect threedimensional corrections into the anterior (e.g., bicoronal)
and posterior (e.g., bilateral lambdoid) regions.

Other Synostoses
Multisutural synostoses are treated in a similar but specific
manner.8,42 The initial design focus of the postoperative

CRO is to correct the deformity directly associated with the
surgically removed suture by maintaining a complete hold
over prominent areas of the skull and proving sufficient void
spaces to overcorrect in areas of flattening where growth was
restricted preoperatively. Symmetry, proportion, and sloping may need to be addressed separately over the course of
treatment to correct the overall skull shape.8,18,25,44 Strategic remolding efforts with the use of consecutive CROs are
designed to overcorrect in the most affected areas of flattening, establish normocephaly as quickly as possible, and then
maintain the skull shape throughout the period of greatest
growth until at least 12 months of age.

Orthotic Treatment Program
The treatment goals of the orthotic program include but are
not limited to improving functional outcomes, aesthetics
(including the improvement of attitudes and perceptions)
and approximating normal size, shape, symmetry, and proportion of the skull. The collaborative surgical and orthotic
treatment program involves the endoscopic removal of
affected sutures, additional surgical procedures as indicated
(e.g., barrel stave osteotomies), orthotic overcorrection of
the skull shape when necessary, stabilization of the remolded
skull, and minimization of immediate postoperative and/or

227

• Fig. 15.14 Postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) design for sagittal synostosis; red line is postoperative head shape; blue line is orthotic design blueprint. (Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

mentioned, CRO design materials and features may change
in order to optimize the skull remolding process and outcomes (Fig. 15.17).

Preoperative Procedures

• Fig. 15.15 Postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) for metopic
synostosis. (Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

late complications. Timelines for the initiation of the orthotic
treatment program vary by surgeon preference, but a general
timeline involves four stages: (1) endoscopic surgery on day
1, (2) patient discharged on days 2 or 3, (3) scanning by cranial clinician on days 3 or 4, and (4) fitting and delivery of
the postoperative CRO by days 7 through 10.18,25,26,29–33
The postoperative scan captures a three-dimensional digital file of the unique contours of the infant’s head. Using
computer-assisted design processes, this digital image is
modified to address the asymmetries, disproportions, sloping, and volume displacements. Modifications and design
specifications are determined by the surgeon and cranial
orthotist relative to the clinical protocols followed by the
medical team. The modified head shape mold is carved
from a rigid foam, and fabrication of the unique and custom CRO continues. By design, the first postoperative
CRO will have a more uncompromising fit for earlier and
faster reshaping of the infant’s head and will last between
4 to 8 weeks depending on a multitude of factors, such as
the infant’s age and rate of cranial growth, severity of head
shape, and adjustability of the orthosis (e.g., contact and
void spaces). Consecutive CROs will be used to further
guide head growth towards normocephaly and enhance
the overall and three-dimensional head shape over time. As

Families benefit from a preoperative visit to the cranial
orthotist for an overview of the orthotic treatment program,
including a preliminary assessment, a preoperative scan or
measurements, an explanation of the anthropometric measurements, understanding of determinants for consecutive
orthoses, and for detailed information about the extended
orthotic treatment program. The cranial orthotist will
outline treatment goals and specifically discuss the wearing schedule and cleaning regimen that must be adhered
to throughout the course of treatment. It is important that
families fully understand the treatment program and commit to the entire process before the surgical procedure. Postoperative orthotic treatment programs require consistent
and frequent follow-up and may continue for a duration
between 6 and 12 months.

Postoperative Procedures
The cranial orthotist will arrange to obtain a postoperative
scan after some of the initial swelling has dissipated. This will
help to optimize the fit and function of the initial orthotic
design acting on the malleable bony structures of the neurocranium. This initial postoperative scan will be used for
fabrication of the first postoperative CRO. A secured digital file of the scan is forwarded to an FDA-cleared manufacturer along with design and modification specifications
determined by the cranial orthotist and craniofacial team.
Fabrication of postoperative designs must be expedited and
the CRO fit as soon as possible.

Initial Fitting
Initial fitting of the orthosis involves critical assessment of the
areas of growth restriction and areas of growth promotion.

• Fig. 15.17 Postoperative orthotic remolding process to correct asymmetry, disproportion and/or slop-

ing and achieve normocephaly. (Reprinted with permission from Jimenez DF, et al. Endoscopy-assisted
early correction of single-suture metopic craniosynostosis: a 19-year experience. J Neurosurg Pediatr.

The orthosis must fit securely on the head and apply slight
compression in strategic areas of the infant’s very flexible
and malleable skull. Trim lines are modified to maintain stabilization of the orthosis on the head, to provide coverage to
bossed areas, and to allow and encourage appropriate neck

range of motion. With slight compressive forces applied to
the skull, the skin will appear slightly pink in these areas
upon removal of the orthosis. This is one means of verifying
correct fit of the orthosis, and the slight discoloration will
fade within a few minutes after removal of the orthosis.

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

229

Caregiver Education
After discharge from the hospital, parents are instructed to
keep the incision dry and to keep baby’s head clean and dry.
They are encouraged to use a perfume free soap and to not
use lotions or powders on the baby’s head. Further instructions for immediate care of the head and incisions are provided by the surgical team.10
During the fitting and delivery appointment for the
CRO, parents receive detailed instructions on proper donning and doffing procedures (Fig. 15.18), the 23-hour per
day wearing schedule, proper cleaning and disinfecting of
the orthosis, skin assessment and care (especially for areas
of contact/compression), and reasons to discontinue use
and contact the orthotist or medical team.10 A brief breakin period is provided to allow the infant to quickly acclimate to full-time wear of the orthosis within 3 to 5 days
after fitting of the orthosis. Caregivers are encouraged to
remove the orthosis several times a day for cleaning, and also
to visually inspect the incision sites. The full-time wearing
schedule is critical to the success of the postoperative CRO
program. One hour off per day is allocated for bathing and
hygiene, along with stringent cleaning and disinfecting of
the orthosis.

Modifications
Brain and skull growth occur quickly, especially after release
of the affected suture. The orthosis must be assessed and
modified in a consistent and timely manner to best address
these three-dimensional head shape changes. Linear measurements are helpful in determining general head shape
changes, and scans provide more detailed volumetric data.
During any of the scheduled visits to the cranial orthotist,
trim lines may be adjusted and strategic padding may be
added and/or material removed to optimize the fit and function of the orthosis (Fig. 15.19). The plastic shell may also
be trimmed or heated and further customized to best meet
the treatment objectives for each patient.

Follow-Up
Follow-up appointments will be scheduled for ongoing
assessment, adjustments, measurements, scans, and modifications. Parents receive detailed instructions regarding situations that need to be immediately reported to the doctor
and medical team. In general, these same conditions apply to
the immediate removal of the orthosis and include nausea,
vomiting, high fever, increased distress, excessive sleepiness,
and increased drainage or swelling at the incision site.10 Any
redness over CRO contact areas that does not fade within
30 to 60 minutes after removal of the orthosis should be
brought to the attention of the orthotist. Follow-up schedules are determined by the treating orthotist and the medical team. It is not uncommon to schedule weekly follow-up
visits for the first 2 to 3 months after fitting the postoperative orthosis to effectively manage and direct the rapid and

• Fig. 15.18 Initial postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) fitting
with family. (Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

substantial brain and skull growth.9–11,14,17–19,25,31,34 After 6
months of age, monthly brain growth slows by half the rate
of the previous 3 months, and follow-up visits may shift to
bi-monthly if appropriate.
During the orthotic treatment program, the infant is
also monitored by the surgical team with ongoing communications and discussions with the orthotist. Most of the
major head shape changes occur within the first few weeks
of orthotic wear, and most correction is obtained by the first
postoperative CRO in the first 2 months of treatment.10,18
The second CRO provides further correction and maintenance to the overall skull shape and will also be used to
address any deformational concerns.

Duration of Orthotic Treatment Program
The duration of orthotic treatment is not standardized as
changes to the neurocranium and viscerocranium occur
at different rates throughout the treatment program. It
is important to remember that the immediate changes
observed after surgery and within the first 6 to 8 weeks of
orthotic treatment are not the final head shape. Most craniofacial teams encourage the use of postoperative CROs
for up to 12 months of age to obtain full correction, and
also to prevent the risk of recurrence of the abnormal head
shape.4,6,9,10,12–15,17,18,24–30,32,34,36 Other factors to consider
for termination of treatment prior to 1 year of age include
the child’s neurodevelopmental level, comorbidities, sutural
involvement, anthropometric measurements and treatment goals, and specific family dynamics or caregiver environments. The decision to discontinue use of the orthosis
is made by the surgeon,17 and the family is instructed to
return to the cranial orthotist for an end of treatment scan.

230 C HA P T ER 1 5

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

A

B
• Fig. 15.19 Modifications to the postoperative cranial remolding orthosis (CRO) are made throughout

the treatment program: (A) to establish and maintain appropriate trimlines, and (B) strategically adding or
removing pads to correct deformity, resist rotation, and prevent translation. (Photos courtesy Orthomerica
Products Inc.)

Final comparative reports are shared with the family and
surgeon, and annual follow-up visits are continued with the
craniofacial team for several years after surgery and discontinuation of the orthosis.

Consecutive CROs
Due to the rapid growth and changing head shape in the
critical postoperative time period, consecutive CROs are
required to capture and direct significant head growth.
The number of CROs used varies between medical teams
and is dependent on a variety of factors. Most craniofacial
teams recognize the need for up to three orthoses, with
most infants requiring two.4,9,11,14,15,17,18,25–28,33,34,44
Very unique and unusual circumstances may require more
than three postoperative CROs to obtain full correction of
the skull deformity and maintenance of the infant’s skull
shape.5,29 It has been reported that the number of CROs
required is inversely proportional to the infant’s age at
the time of surgery.18,29 Infants closer to 2 months of age
at the time of surgery are more likely to need additional
CROs than infants undergoing surgical procedures from
3 to 6 months of age. Again, this is due to the normal
and anticipated brain growth patterns after the removal
of the sutural growth restriction. Indications for a second orthosis include loss of purchase under the mastoid
regions and no further adjustments above the ears in the
coronal plane. Ultimately, the number of postoperative
CROs needed is also influenced by compliance, surgical
technique, rate of growth, orthotic design, and clinical
fitting techniques, and must be determined on an individual basis.

Compliance
Compliance with the orthotic remolding program is critical
to support the surgical corrections and guide further skull
shape changes. Optimal outcomes have been reported by
many craniofacial teams using endoscopic procedures with
postoperative CROs on young patients with fully compliant parents.11,15,23,26,32,38 Preoperative family education,
discussions, and commitments to the postoperative CRO
program are critical to the success of the endoscopic surgical procedure.4,8,15,27,36 A small percentage of caregivers
report noncompliance reasons such as perceived discomfort, hindrance, inconvenience of follow-up appointments,
skin rash, and disruptions to cuddling or handling of the
infants.4,13,32 Failure to comply with the orthotic treatment program compromises both the surgical and orthotic
outcomes as it allows circumferential growth of the skull
with little to no actual change in head shape. Growth over
prominent areas is an indication of noncompliance and the
flattened areas will not respond with specific and directed
growth. Overwhelmingly, the orthosis is well-received by
most parents and infants and the treatment program is completed with few if any issues.7,8,13,18,38

Complications/Adverse Events
Complications are infrequently reported with the use of
postoperative CROs; several studies report no complications
with any of the initial or consecutive CROs.5,9,13,15,17,18,26
Concerns noted include mild skin irritation, superficial
skin breakdown, excessive moisture inside the orthosis or
sweating, and recurrent scabbing of the incision.5,15,17,26

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 15

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

231

A strategic break-in wearing period, frequent removal and
inspection of the skin by the parent, focused cleaning and
disinfecting of the orthosis, and ongoing clinical followup and adjustments will enhance the wearing program. In
some cases, the orthosis may be designed or modified with
ventilation holes to increase the air circulation within the
orthosis and decrease excessive heat and perspiration. The
orthotic remolding process involves restricted dimensional
growth that will produce resistant pressures. The skin will
acclimate to these pressures as continued brain growth follows the path of least resistance into the void areas designed
inside the orthosis. Proper fit and function of the orthosis
must be verified and modified at strategic intervals. Delayed
follow-up and adjustments may result in loss of stabilization of the orthosis on the head and compromise outcomes.
Clinical protocols developed specifically for the provision
of endoscopic and postoperative CRO programs will avoid
and/or lessen potential complications or adverse events.

Anthropometric Measurements and Scanning
Technology
Anthropometric measurements are taken at specified time
intervals to track both linear and volumetric changes in
skull shape.8,10,17,18,25,30,31,34 Manual measurements are
often a quick and easy means of tracking general changes
and trends, although the precision of capturing these
dimensional measurements on an active and potentially
uncooperative patient may be less consistent. Scanning
technologies allow improved accuracy of anthropometric
measurements over manual measurements. Scan acquisition
may be as short as 2 to 3 seconds, and some scanning systems even allow the infant to sit or be held in the comfort
and security of the caregiver’s lap during the scanning process (Fig. 15.20). Scans provide the added benefits of additional measures, such as volumes, quadrant assessments,
global symmetry, and proportion comparisons that extend
beyond simple linear measures on the three-dimensional
infant skull. It is important to note that two-dimensional
measurements of three-dimensional deformities fail to capture the magnitude of the deformity or the magnitude of
the surgical and/or orthotic correction.14 Professional consensus has not been reached on appropriate measures to
track or document progress, full correction, clinical success,
or even normocephaly.21 However, craniofacial teams do
recognize specific measures of improved symmetry, proportion, and overall head shape that continue to guide clinical
treatment objectives.
Quantitative measures obtained are based on anthropometric landmarks such as the glabella, opisthocranion,
eurion, frontozygomaticus, nasion, sellion, tragion, and
other anatomical landmarks to provide linear dimensions
of cranial width, cranial length, transcranial diameter distances, and cranial height. Standard cephalic index measures may not be as useful in scaphocephalic head shapes
as the eurions may be displaced. Normative cephalic index
data has not been agreed upon.5,29,43 Dated cephalic index

• Fig. 15.20 Portable SmartSoc scanning system for cranial remolding
orthoses. (Photo courtesy Orthomerica Products Inc.)

charts developed for prone sleepers are not likely relevant
to supine sleeping infants. Additionally, unique deformations found in metopic (trigonocephaly) and unicoronal
synostosis (plagiocephaly with trapezoid distortion) are
not reflected in standardized skull measurements.7 Both
quantitative measures and qualitative observations should
be used to direct ongoing treatment and document clinical
outcomes. Postoperative scans are often scheduled within
the first few days after surgery, and then approximately at
1, 3, 6, and 9 months throughout the orthotic treatment
program.10,12,34 Scan reports allow frequent assessment
of available and relevant linear and volumetric measurements over time along with the qualitative assessment of
the infant’s skull. A final scan is also acquired at discharge
from the CRO treatment program. Craniofacial teams
may continue to track the corrected head shape with long
term scans up to 5 years of age.

Clinical Outcomes
Excellent clinical and functional outcomes are obtained with
well-fitting CROs. The definition of successful outcomes
has yet to reach professional consensus. Many authors report
significant qualitative and quantitative improvements via
clinical inspection and tracking of anthropometric measurements.5,9–12,17,18,22,24,28,38,30,45 Again, without agreement on
normocephaly for today’s supine sleeping infants, clinical
outcomes and definitions of success are varied and difficult
to compare. Clinical results and criteria for endoscopic corrections and postoperative CRO programs are summarized
in Table 15.1. The literature is challenging as it lacks definitions (e.g., normocephaly), consistency of terminology (e.g.,
cephalic ratio, cephalic index, adjusted cephalic index), and
reports on a variety of anthropometric measurements and
techniques (e.g., CT scans vs. laser scans vs. manual measurements). There are also difficulties in determining the

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy
235

booksmedicos.org

236 C HA P T ER 1 5

Postoperative Cranial Orthotic Therapy

precise timelines for data points reported at “postoperative,”
“discontinuation of CRO,” and “end of treatment” periods.
The decision to discontinue the orthotic treatment program
is often made based upon visual inspection (e.g., excellent
results or high levels of satisfaction), predetermined age (e.g.,
up to 12 months of age), or simplistic linear measurements
(e.g., cephalic index) that fail to describe the magnitude of
the three-dimensional deformities or corrections. Anthropometric measurements used for deformational plagiocephaly,
such as cranial vault asymmetry or cephalic index, are not
often directly applicable to synostotic conditions. Until
defined methods of measurement and unique anthropometric measurements are developed to address the unique
presentations of the various forms of craniosynostosis, difficulties will remain in the comparison of clinical outcomes.
There is significant agreement that the clinical outcomes
obtained for younger infants with endoscopic procedures
and the use of postoperative CROs are comparable to open
cranial vault procedures for older patients.4,9,11,29,38,30,34
Efforts to identify craniosynostosis in very young infants
to capitalize on skull malleability after suture removal and
optimal growth patterns during the orthotic treatment programs offers an option to open cranial vault procedures
with excellent results. Early intervention in this manner also
allows normal growth patterns to resume for further corrections and improved outcomes. Key factors in obtaining
excellent results include the age of the infant at the time
of surgery, surgical procedures, orthotic design features,
skills and experience of the cranial orthotist, compliance
with the wearing schedule, and appropriate duration of the
orthotic treatment program in order to obtain longstanding
corrections.9,10,24,37

Regression After CRO Treatment
The desired head shape must be obtained and maintained
for an appropriate period of time before the orthosis is discontinued as dural forces may affect a negative and undesirable force to return the head to the preoperative shape.
As discussed, a small regression in cephalic index often
occurs in scaphocephalic head shapes so initial overcorrection is recommended.18 It has been noted that either failure
to follow the CRO protocol, early discontinuation of the
orthotic treatment program, or ineffective fit and function
of the orthosis may produce suboptimal corrections and
clinical outcomes.9–11,16,28,29 Cranial orthotists with specialized training and expertise are key medical team members
for postoperative endoscopic treatment programs. Ongoing
communications and structured follow-up with the surgical
team and cranial orthotist will address caregiver concerns
and support any wavering commitment to the orthotic
treatment program.

Conclusion
CROs allow for “directed growth” and are thus critical
to the success of endoscopic surgical procedures for the

treatment of craniosynostosis. Postoperative CROs are currently recommended as standard treatment and adjuncts to
most endoscopic and some open cranial vault surgical procedures. Craniofacial programs are encouraged to partner
with an experienced cranial orthotist and develop a structured postoperative orthotic program that best meets the
needs of the patient and medical team. Successful outcomes
with CROs are dependent on many factors including but
not limited to age of the infant, affected suture(s), modifications and design of the cranial orthosis, skills of the cranial
orthotist, ongoing adjustments to optimize fit and function
of the orthosis, communication and collaboration of the
entire medical team, and the caregiver’s compliance with
the wearing schedule and recommended duration of the
orthotic treatment program. The combination of the cranial orthotist’s experience, postoperative orthotic materials
and design features, and clinic protocols have been shown
to enhance the surgical results for infants with craniosynostosis. The ongoing advancement of orthotic designs and
treatment programs relies on professional consensus and
the standardization of terminology, anatomical landmarks,
and anthropometric measurements that relate to the threedimensional evaluations and corrections of skull deformities. Additional measurements are available from a variety
of scanning technologies commonly used in orthotic clinics, and the craniofacial team should take advantage of this
detailed information to further direct and appropriately
time the discharge from the orthotic treatment programs.
Ongoing communication between the multidisciplinary
team is critical to the success of the postoperative orthotic
treatment program after endoscopic surgical procedures.

16

Complications and
Avoidance
DAVID F. JIMENEZ, MD, FACS

Introduction
Review of complications associated with the surgical treatment of craniosynostosis varies widely due to reports that
extend over many years and epochs, as well as there being a
large number of different procedures being reported during
these periods. A 2015 publication in the Journal of Neurosurgery by Lin Y, et al.,1 analyzed and compared the complication rates of patients under 12 months of age undergoing
treatment for nonsyndromic craniosynostosis by two groups
as well as a review of the current literature. The groups analyzed included a large administrative claims database (KID:
Kid’s Inpatient database) and a clinical registry of the American College of Surgeons (Peds NSQIP: Pediatric National
Surgical Quality Improvement Program). Also included was
a review of the literature current up to that time. The complications identified were grouped into seven major categories: infection, wound disruption, seizures, cardiac events,
strokes and/or intracranial hemorrhage, blood transfusions,
and sepsis. Results are presented in Table 16.1. This chapter
also presents the complications associated with the cohort of
nonsyndromic craniosynostosis treated by our team during
the past 25 years using endoscopic techniques. Our results
are summarized in Table 16.2. We do not consider a blood
transfusion to be a complication but report it for comparison purposes. Asides from presenting our complication rate,
we also present our perspective on causes as well management principles.

Dural Tears
The most common unexpected complication is our series
was a dural tear with an overall rate of 0.9%. This problem occurred in 9 patients. Four sagittal, three coronal,
one bicoronal, and one metopic patient were affected.
Three of the dural tears were due to the cranial perforator
being advanced passed the skull’s inner table and cutting
the underlying dura matter. In every case, the dura was
successfully repaired (Fig. 16.1). In the sagittal group, a

large dural cut occurred in one 4-month-old male when
the left paramedian osteotomy was being made with the
Mayo scissors. It led to a 4-cm dural cut with breach of
the cerebral cortex, whose bleeding was easily managed
with bipolar coagulation and Surgiflo. In order to fully
repair the tear, more cranium was removed laterally so as
to increase dural exposure. The repair was done with 4-0
Neurolon and under direct endoscopic illumination and
visualization. Subsequent sequela included a cerebrospinal
fluid (CSF) leak, pseudomeningocele, and, later, a secondary repair. The last dural tear was a small dural cut (≈1 cm)
that occurred while doing a wedge osteotomy which was
placed too close to the right coronal suture. This tear was
similarly repaired with no lasting consequences. In the coronal cohort, there were three tears when the osteotomies
were made very near the open end of the coronal suture in
close proximity to the pterion and behind the greater wing
of the sphenoid wing. A similar situation occurred in one
bicoronal patient that required a second procedure to
repair the tear and stop the CSF leak and pseudomeningocele progression. A very small tear of the dura lateral to the
anterior sagittal sinus occurred in a single metopic patient,
which was repaired with two single Neurolon sutures.
No further complications were associated with this small
tear. No dural lacerations occurred in the lambdoid suture
releases. None of the dural tears in our series resulted in
neurologic problems or complications. None of the dural
tear repairs required extending the surgery to an open procedure in order to repair the tear.

Avoidance Pearls/Tips
• E
 ducate assistants and trainees on the importance of
proper hand pressure application to the craniotome when
using it in young infants with very thin skulls. Light and
constant pressure is required and willingness to stop at
any time that “it doesn’t feel right.” Unlike adults, when
the burr hole is not completed to full thickness, it is relatively easy to finish it with curettes and rongeurs.

an = 837.

markedly increasing the chances for a dural tear. This can
have terrible consequences as when a cut into the transverse or sigmoid sinus occurs and can lead to massive
blood loss, brain ischemia, and severe neurologic damage
(personal communication).
• When developing the anterior and posterior osteotomies
in sagittal patients, it is imperative that the sagittal sinus
be totally separated from the overlying bone prior to the
osteotomy. This can be easily done by gently and carefully advancing a #1 Penfield dissector (wide rounded
end) across the sinus. When making the posterior osteotomy in front of the lambda, the same technique is used.
This area carries significant risk as it is often seen that the
stenosed suture also has a ridge that extends inward and
indents the underlying sagittal sinus.
• In coronal synostosis, the osteotomy should be made
behind the stenosed suture, as discussed in the coronal
chapter, in order to avoid the always-present open coronal suture near the pterion.
• Full and complete release and visualization of the dura
at and near the asterion (when releasing a lambdoid
suture) is imperative in order to decrease the risk of
severe bleeding due to injury to the transverse or sigmoid sinus.

Infections

• U
 tmost care must be taken to make sure that the dura
has been fully separated from the cranium prior to making the osteotomy. The 30-degree endoscope should be
advanced the entire length of the planned osteotomy site.
When making the bony cuts, make sure that the tips of
the scissors are pointing upward in order to avoid catching the dura with the scissors.
• Avoid making osteotomies near the open sutures such
as the coronal or lambdoid sutures. The sharpie fibers
of a patent suture tightly adhere the dura to the bone,

a visible suture is seen, it may be easily removed. The patient
is placed on a 7-day regimen of oral prophylactic antibiotic

Avoidance Pearls/Tips
• W
 hen closing the scalp, the absorbable galeal sutures
should be placed in the galea. The tendency is to place
and bring the suture close to the dermis in order to bring
the epidermis together. This places the suture too close to
the surface and increases the risk of an abscess.
• When cutting the suture, the assistant should cut as close
to the suture knot as possible. Leaving a suture tail will
lead it to protrude thru the skin and cause an abscess.
• Intravenous antibiotics should be given 30 minutes prior
to making the first skin incision.
• Copious irrigation of the surgical field with an antibiotic
saline solution helps to wash the debris away and keep
the surgical field clean.
• Fig. 16.6 The dermatitis spread to the forehead and area of the

incision, which ultimately led to a wound infection, even with removal
of helmet use.

Five patients (two metopic, two sagittal, one coronal)

to 10 minutes several times a day. In all cases the dermatitis resolved. Two patients developed a second bout of early
erythema following replacement of the helmet. In those

Two patients sustained inadvertent scalp burns that required
secondary surgeries to correct. In both situations the burn
was caused by the faulty transmission of monopolar current through newly obtained insulated and coated instruments. In one coronal case, the cauterizing electricity was
conducted by an insulated suction instrument that was in
direct contact with the skin/galea complex while the bone
was being cauterized in the coagulation mode set at 60
watts. Although the sin looked somewhat blanched at the
time of surgery, the area began to develop a thick eschar
over the ensuing weeks (Fig. 16.10). Removal of the eschar
revealed necrotic scalp at the posterior end of the incision as
well as the area burned by the suction (Fig. 16.11). When
taken to surgery, a noninfected, full-thickness burn was seen
(Fig. 16.12). A wide excision was undertaken of the burned
area (Fig. 16.13). The incision was successfully closed and
no long-lasting sequelae were seen at a 6-month follow-up
(Fig. 16.14).

Avoidance Pearls/Tips
• E
 xtreme care should be taken when manipulating the
suction-coagulator (set at 60 watts) for osseous coagulation. The J&B dural retractor, which was designed and
developed with the idea of protecting the dura and galea
simultaneously when coagulating bone, should be used
in most cases.
• If scalp vein/artery bleeding is encountered, judicious use
of bipolar cautery should be done. Resort to Surgiflo,
gelfoam, or Surgicel and direct pressure to decrease the
risk of scalp burn.

Pseudomeningocele
There were four cases of significant pseudomeningocele formation secondary to CSF leak into the subgaleal space. Two
cases occurred in infants with metopic stenosis who were
found to have significant bony ridges indenting into the
dura and thinning it out. In both cases the area of concern
was near the glabella. CSF was seen at the time of surgery
and dural sutures were placed. Following surgery, the pseudomeningocele developed on the first postoperative day
and continued to enlarge (Fig. 16.16). The first patient was
managed with a lumbar drain which was left indwelling for

endoscopic treatment for metopic craniosynostosis. The patient was
successfully managed with a lumbar drain.

and had fully resolved by the fifth day of drainage. No further treatment was necessary. The second patient was managed successfully as well with two large-volume lumbar taps,

Cranial Defects

tex defect which we suspected would not ossify secondary to use of
allograft dural repair.

Review of the endoscopic literature reveals the occurrence
of cranial defects anytime the osteogenic dura is manipulated. It may lead to damage of the osteoprogenitor cells,
which in turn can lead to unwanted cranial defects. As
previously stated in other chapters, we strongly believe
that the dura needs to be managed with utmost respect
and manipulation and cauterization should be kept to an
absolute minimum. We have empirically observed that the
dura over the vertex tends to ossify somewhat differently
than the dura over/near the metopic and coronal sutures.
As such, our metopic and coronal osteotomies are only
4 to 5 mm in width. When we began treating metopic
synostosis, our osteotomies were as wide as 2 cm and it
became very evident that ossification took longer than over
the sagittal suture area. We therefore decreased the width
to 4- or 5-mm. Large defects that may require repair can
be seen with wide craniectomies (Fig. 16.21) as seen from
this outside case at another institution. If the cranial defect
is small, size of 1 cm or less and behind the hairline, it has

been our policy not to treat. We have been careful and fortunate not to have had any defects over the forehead where
a visible cosmetic problem may arise. One 2-cm, visibly
pulsatile defect surrounding the ventricular shunt catheter
entrance of a sagittal patient that developed hydrocephalus
has recently developed and parents have decided to have
it repaired and plans are set to do so in the near future.
Similarly, two male sagittal synostosis patients developed
small cranial defects directly over the sagittal sinus and out
of an abundance of caution, both were repaired with same
day cranioplasties. We have only had one large vertex cranial defect that developed after a large parasagittal dural
tear and a pseudomeningocele. For adequate and complete
closure, an allograft duraplasty was used (Fig. 16.22) and,
expecting a large defect to develop, a titanium cranioplasty
was placed over the defect (Figs. 16.23 and 16.24). Six
years after the surgery, the patient has done well and the
titanium cranioplasty has incorporated well into the surrounding bone.

booksmedicos.org

CHAPTER 16

• Fig. 16.24 Postoperative side view of patient showing resolution of
the pseudomeningocele and a normal head contour. Follow-up 6 years
later showed persistent normalization of head shape with no cranial
defects.

Avoidance Pearls/Tips
• S mall calvarial defects behind the hairline can be managed successfully in an expectant manner.
• When dural bleeding is encountered, hemostatic agents
such as Surgiflo, gelfoam, Surgicel, or Avistat should be
generously used.
• Coagulation of the dura should not be done except to
coagulate bridging veins. Cauterizing bleeding dura will
only lead to dural defects.
• Pseudomeningoceles should be promptly addressed as
well as infections, which may lead to dural damage and
subsequent dural defects.

Deaths
The overall mortality rate in our series is 0.25% and consists
of two infants with sagittal synostosis. In neither case was the
death directly related to the surgical procedure itself but was
associated with other factors. The first case was a 3-month-old
infant without any history of bleeding or coagulation disorders. The surgical time was 36 minutes, estimated blood loss
was 10 mL. There were no intraoperative problems of any

Complications and Avoidance

247

kind. Being the first case of the day, the child reached the
pediatric intensive care unit (PICU) at 10:20 a.m. in stable
condition and neurologically intact. Normal blood pressures
and O2 saturations were observed until 3:40 p.m., when the
saturation began to drop precipitously. The child became
unresponsive and required emergent intubation and ventilator support. A stat computed tomography (CT) scan did not
show intracranial hemorrhages or hematomas. The patient’s
hemoglobin began to drop rapidly as well and required
a transfusion. A blood smear analysis and other work-up
showed massive red blood cell hemolysis and destruction,
which led to severe and irreversible cerebral ischemia, as documented by follow-up scans. Because of the extremely poor
neurologic prognosis, parents withdrew all care. An extensive
institutional Root Cause Analysis failed to pinpoint the specific etiology of the hemolytic breakdown. An enzyme deficiency was strongly suspected.
The second case occurred in a 2-month-old male. Surgery was totally uneventful with a surgical time of 41 minutes, with a blood loss of 30 mL. In a similar fashion as the
previous patient, he reached the PICU in stable condition
and without problems. On the evening of the surgery, he
became unresponsive and required emergent intubation.
The head CT showed no abnormalities. He began to desaturate significantly and stopped producing urine. Further
diagnostic work-up showed that he had begun to embolize
clots throughout his body, including brain, kidneys, lungs,
and intestines. CT scan of the head showed severe ischemic
injury and damage, leading the parents to withdraw care.
We do not believe that these deaths could have been prevented, unless a very extensive blood work was done preoperatively looking for very uncommon abnormalities, which
is not standard of care nor indicated.

Conclusion
Review of our series of endoscopic treated patients, which is
the world’s largest and with the longest follow-up, demonstrates that these are extremely safe procedures with minimal rates of complications. As shown in previous chapters,
long-term results are excellent across all sutures.
